<a id='question-086'></a>

### Question 086: Pes Anserine Bursitis
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Identify the anatomical structures located deep to the pes anserinus bursa and the nerve most susceptible to compression or irritation in this region. (识别鹅足囊深层解剖结构以及该区域最易受压迫或刺激的神经。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 58-year-old obese female presents to her primary care physician complaining of pain localized to the anteromedial aspect of her proximal tibia. The pain has been gradually worsening over the past several months, exacerbated by walking and stair climbing. She has a history of osteoarthritis in both knees. Physical examination reveals tenderness to palpation directly over the proximal tibia, approximately 5 cm inferior to the medial joint line. The patient reports numbness and tingling radiating down the medial aspect of her lower leg when pressure is applied over the tender area. An MRI confirms inflammation of the bursa overlying the proximal tibia in the described location. Which of the following structures is most likely affected by the inflammation and compression in this clinical scenario?

(A) Tibial nerve
(B) Saphenous nerve
(C) Sural nerve
(D) Common fibular (peroneal) nerve
(E) Superficial fibular (peroneal) nerve

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 58-year-old obese female with osteoarthritis. Obesity and osteoarthritis are risk factors for pes anserine bursitis due to altered biomechanics and increased stress on the medial knee/proximal tibia.
- **Location of Pain:** Anteromedial aspect of the proximal tibia, 5 cm inferior to the medial joint line. This is the classic location of the pes anserinus bursa.
- **Exacerbating Factors:** Walking and stair climbing. These activities increase stress on the medial knee and proximal tibia, irritating the inflamed bursa.
- **Physical Exam Findings:** Tenderness over the proximal tibia in the pes anserinus region.
- **Neurological Symptoms:** Numbness and tingling radiating down the medial aspect of the lower leg upon pressure over the tender area. This suggests compression or irritation of a nerve located deep to or in close proximity to the inflamed bursa.
- **Imaging:** MRI confirms inflammation of the bursa in the pes anserinus region.
- **Question Stem:** Asks which structure is most likely affected by the inflammation and compression.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Pes Anserinus Bursa:** A large, subcutaneous bursa located between the tendons of the sartorius, gracilis, and semitendinosus muscles (the "pes anserinus" or goose foot) and the medial aspect of the proximal tibia.
- **Anatomical Relationships:** The pes anserinus bursa lies superficial to the medial tibial plateau and the medial tibial condyle. Deep to the bursa and medial to the tibia lies the **saphenous nerve** and the **saphenous artery and vein**. The tibial nerve runs deeper and more posteriorly, medial to the tibial artery. The sural nerve is located more laterally and posteriorly. The common fibular nerve branches in the fibular head region. The superficial fibular nerve runs laterally around the fibular neck.
- **Innervation:** The saphenous nerve is the largest cutaneous branch of the femoral nerve. It provides sensory innervation to the medial aspect of the leg and the medial side of the foot.
- **Clinical Correlation:** Inflammation of the pes anserinus bursa (pes anserine bursitis) can cause pain, swelling, and tenderness in the anteromedial proximal tibia. Due to its close proximity, the saphenous nerve can be compressed or irritated by the inflamed bursa or the surrounding soft tissue swelling, leading to paresthesias (numbness, tingling) along its distribution (medial leg and foot). The patient's symptoms of numbness and tingling radiating down the medial aspect of her lower leg upon palpation strongly suggest involvement of the saphenous nerve.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Tibial nerve:** The tibial nerve runs deeper and more posteriorly than the pes anserinus bursa, medial to the tibial artery. While it innervates the posterior compartment of the leg and the sole of the foot, it is not typically affected by pes anserine bursitis. Its distribution is primarily posterior.
- (C) **Sural nerve:** The sural nerve is formed by branches of the tibial and common fibular nerves. It runs down the posterior aspect of the calf, lateral to the Achilles tendon, and provides sensory innervation to the posterolateral aspect of the lower leg and the lateral side of the foot. It is located far from the pes anserinus bursa.
- (D) **Common fibular (peroneal) nerve:** This nerve branches off the sciatic nerve in the popliteal fossa and wraps around the fibular head. It innervates the lateral compartment of the leg and provides sensory innervation to the dorsum of the foot. It is located laterally and superiorly to the pes anserinus bursa.
- (E) **Superficial fibular (peroneal) nerve:** A branch of the common fibular nerve, it runs down the lateral aspect of the leg, providing sensory innervation to the anterolateral aspect of the lower leg and the dorsum of the foot. It is located laterally and superiorly to the pes anserinus bursa.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Structure                 | Location Relative to Pes Anserinus Bursa | Innervation/Function                                  | Relevance to Pes Anserine Bursitis |
| :------------------------ | :--------------------------------------- | :---------------------------------------------------- | :-------------------------------- |
| **Saphenous Nerve (Correct)** | Deep to the bursa, medial to tibia       | Sensory: Medial leg and foot (from femoral nerve) | **Most likely compressed/irritated** |
| Tibial Nerve              | Deeper, posterior to bursa, medial to tibia | Motor: Posterior leg; Sensory: Sole of foot          | Unlikely affected                  |
| Sural Nerve               | Posterior, lateral calf                   | Sensory: Posterolateral leg, lateral foot             | Unlikely affected                  |
| Common Fibular Nerve      | Lateral, superior (fibular head)         | Motor: Lateral leg; Sensory: Dorsum foot              | Unlikely affected                  |
| Superficial Fibular Nerve | Lateral leg                              | Sensory: Anterolateral leg, dorsum foot               | Unlikely affected                  |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Pes anserine bursitis involves inflammation of the bursa located deep to the conjoined tendons of sartorius, gracilis, and semitendinosus on the anteromedial proximal tibia. The saphenous nerve, a branch of the femoral nerve, runs deep to this bursa and is susceptible to compression, causing sensory symptoms along the medial leg. Remember the anatomical relationships in this region to differentiate pes anserine bursitis symptoms from other knee/leg pathologies. Key differentials include medial collateral ligament (MCL) sprain (pain more localized to joint line, instability), medial tibial stress syndrome (pain more diffuse along tibial shaft), and osteoarthritis (joint pain, stiffness).

<a id='question-087'></a>

### Question 087: Genu Varum (Bow-Legs) vs Genu Valgum (Knock-Knees) Biomechanics
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the biomechanical forces acting on the knee joint and how they relate to the alignment of the femur and tibia, specifically differentiating genu varum (bow-leggedness) and genu valgum (knock-knees) and their associated stresses. (理解作用于膝关节的生物力学以及它们与股骨和胫骨排列的关系，具体区分 genu varum（O 型腿）和 genu valgum（X 型腿）及其相关的应力。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 6-year-old boy presents to the pediatric orthopedic clinic with his parents concerned about his leg alignment. His parents report that he has always walked with his knees pointing inward (knock-knees). Physical examination reveals a significant intercondylar angle, with the medial femoral condyles appearing closer together than the lateral condyles when the patient stands. Palpation of the medial knee reveals tenderness over the proximal tibia, specifically along the course of the pes anserinus bursa. Radiographs confirm genu valgum with an increased Q-angle. The physician explains that this alignment places increased stress on specific structures around the knee. Which of the following structures is MOST likely to be affected by the increased medial stress associated with this patient's condition?

(A) Lateral collateral ligament (LCL)
(B) Medial collateral ligament (MCL)
(C) Lateral meniscus
(D) Anterior cruciate ligament (ACL)
(E) Pes anserinus tendons

#### Correct Answer: (B)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 6-year-old boy with knock-knees (genu valgum). This is a common developmental condition in young children.
- **Physical Exam:** Significant intercondylar angle (knees pointing inward), tenderness over the proximal medial tibia along the pes anserinus bursa.
- **Radiographs:** Confirm genu valgum, increased Q-angle.
- **Pathophysiology:** Genu valgum means the knees angle inward, causing the medial side of the knee joint to bear increased weight and stress. The increased Q-angle (angle between the quadriceps tendon and the patellar tendon) also contributes to increased medial stress on the knee.
- **Question Stem:** Asks which structure is MOST likely affected by the increased *medial* stress.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Genu Valgum:** Characterized by the medial femoral condyles being closer together than the lateral condyles. This alignment shifts the center of gravity laterally relative to the knee joint, increasing the valgus stress (force pushing the knee inward) on the medial compartment.
- **Medial Knee Structures:** The medial compartment includes the medial femoral condyle, medial tibial plateau, medial meniscus, medial collateral ligament (MCL), and the pes anserinus tendons (sartorius, gracilis, semitendinosus).
- **Increased Medial Stress:** In genu valgum, the medial compartment experiences increased compressive and tensile forces.
    - **MCL:** The MCL is the primary stabilizer against valgus stress. Increased valgus stress directly increases tension on the MCL, making it prone to strain or injury.
    - **Medial Meniscus:** Increased compression in the medial compartment can increase the risk of medial meniscus tears.
    - **Pes Anserinus Tendons:** These tendons insert onto the medial aspect of the proximal tibia, just below the medial joint line. Increased stress and friction in this area can lead to pes anserinus bursitis or tendinopathy, consistent with the patient's tenderness.
- **Lateral Knee Structures:** The lateral compartment includes the lateral femoral condyle, lateral tibial plateau, lateral meniscus, and lateral collateral ligament (LCL). In genu valgum, the lateral compartment experiences *decreased* stress.
- **ACL:** The ACL primarily resists anterior translation of the tibia relative to the femur and rotational forces. While knee alignment can influence ACL stress, the primary effect of genu valgum is increased medial compartment stress.
- **Comparing Options:**
    - (A) LCL: Located on the lateral side, experiences decreased stress in genu valgum.
    - (B) MCL: Located on the medial side, directly resists valgus stress, and is therefore highly susceptible to increased stress in genu valgum.
    - (C) Lateral meniscus: Located on the lateral side, experiences decreased stress in genu valgum.
    - (D) ACL: Primarily resists anterior translation; medial stress is not its primary function.
    - (E) Pes anserinus tendons: Located medially, can be affected by increased stress and friction, but the MCL is the primary ligamentous structure resisting the valgus force itself. However, the tenderness described points towards pes anserinus involvement, which is also a consequence of the altered biomechanics. Between MCL and Pes Anserinus, the MCL is the structure *most directly* stressed by the valgus force itself. The question asks which structure is MOST likely affected by the *increased medial stress*. The MCL is the primary ligament resisting valgus stress.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Lateral collateral ligament (LCL): The LCL is located on the lateral aspect of the knee and resists varus stress (force pushing the knee outward). In genu valgum (knock-knees), the stress is directed medially (valgus), so the LCL experiences *decreased* stress, not increased. This option is incorrect.
- (C) Lateral meniscus: The lateral meniscus is located within the lateral compartment of the knee. Similar to the LCL, the lateral compartment experiences *decreased* stress in genu valgum. This option is incorrect.
- (D) Anterior cruciate ligament (ACL): The ACL primarily resists anterior tibial translation and internal rotation. While knee alignment can influence ACL loading, the primary biomechanical consequence of genu valgum is increased valgus stress on the medial compartment structures, particularly the MCL. The ACL is not the structure most directly affected by this specific type of stress. This option is incorrect.
- (E) Pes anserinus tendons: These tendons insert medially on the proximal tibia. While increased stress and friction in the medial compartment due to genu valgum can lead to pes anserinus bursitis or tendinopathy (consistent with the patient's tenderness), the Medial Collateral Ligament (MCL) is the primary structure responsible for resisting the valgus force itself. Therefore, the MCL is arguably *more* directly affected by the increased medial stress than the pes anserinus tendons, although both can be affected. However, the question asks which structure is MOST likely affected by the *increased medial stress*. The MCL is the primary ligament resisting valgus stress.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Structure             | Location        | Primary Function                                  | Stress in Genu Valgum | Stress in Genu Varum |
|----------------------|-----------------|---------------------------------------------------|-----------------------|----------------------|
| Medial Collateral Ligament (MCL) | Medial Side    | Resists Valgus Stress (knee inward)             | Increased             | Decreased            |
| Lateral Collateral Ligament (LCL) | Lateral Side   | Resists Varus Stress (knee outward)              | Decreased             | Increased            |
| Medial Meniscus       | Medial Side    | Shock absorption, stability                      | Increased             | Decreased            |
| Lateral Meniscus      | Lateral Side   | Shock absorption, stability                      | Decreased             | Increased            |
| Pes Anserinus Tendons | Medial Side    | Hip flexion, knee flexion, internal rotation (hip) | Increased (friction)  | Decreased            |
| Anterior Cruciate Ligament (ACL) | Center        | Resists anterior tibial translation, rotation | Variable, complex    | Variable, complex   |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Genu valgum (knock-knees) increases valgus stress on the medial knee compartment. The Medial Collateral Ligament (MCL) is the primary structure resisting valgus stress and is therefore most susceptible to injury or strain in this condition. Genu varum (bow-legs) increases varus stress on the lateral knee compartment. Key differentials include understanding the function of ligaments (MCL vs LCL), menisci, and tendons (pes anserinus) and how they relate to specific knee stresses.

<a id='question-088'></a>

### Question 088: Anterior Compartment of the Leg: Tibialis Anterior, Extensor Hallucis Longus, Extensor Digitorum Longus, Fibularis Tertius
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The anatomical relationships and innervation of the muscles within the anterior compartment of the leg, specifically the deep peroneal (fibular) nerve's course and the structures it innervates. (下胫神经在腿前间室内的走行及其支配的结构)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male presents to the emergency department after sustaining a closed fracture of the distal third of his left tibia during a fall. Initial management included closed reduction and casting. Three weeks later, he reports persistent foot drop and numbness over the dorsum of his left foot and the web space between his first and second toes. Physical examination reveals weakness in dorsiflexion of the foot and extension of the great toe (MRC grade 3/5). Sensory examination shows decreased sensation in the distribution of the superficial peroneal nerve and the area between the first and second toes. Ankle reflexes are normal. Radiographs show adequate alignment of the fracture. Which of the following structures is most likely compromised due to the initial injury and subsequent casting?

(A) Tibialis Posterior muscle
(B) Sural nerve
(C) Anterior Tibial Artery
(D) Deep Peroneal (Fibular) Nerve
(E) Saphenous nerve

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 62-year-old male with a closed distal tibial fracture. This sets the stage for potential nerve or vascular injury related to the fracture or its management (casting).
- **Symptoms:** Foot drop (inability to dorsiflex the foot) and numbness over the dorsum of the foot and the web space between the first and second toes. Foot drop specifically points to weakness of the dorsiflexors. Numbness in the dorsum and first web space points to sensory loss in the distribution of the deep peroneal nerve.
- **Physical Examination:** Weakness in dorsiflexion (tibialis anterior) and great toe extension (extensor hallucis longus), both innervated by the deep peroneal nerve. Sensory loss confirms the distribution of the deep peroneal nerve. Normal ankle reflexes suggest the tibial nerve is likely intact.
- **Imaging:** Radiographs show fracture alignment, suggesting the primary issue is not malunion or displacement causing ongoing compression, but rather direct injury or entrapment.
- **Conclusion:** The combination of foot drop, weakness in specific anterior compartment muscles (tibialis anterior, extensor hallucis longus), and sensory loss in the characteristic distribution strongly implicates injury to the deep peroneal nerve. The fracture location (distal third of tibia) and subsequent casting are common causes of such nerve injury.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Anterior Compartment Muscles:** The anterior compartment of the leg contains the tibialis anterior, extensor hallucis longus, extensor digitorum longus, and fibularis (peroneus) tertius muscles. These muscles are responsible for dorsiflexion of the foot and extension of the toes.
- **Innervation:** All muscles in the anterior compartment are innervated by the deep peroneal (fibular) nerve, a branch of the common peroneal (fibular) nerve.
- **Deep Peroneal Nerve Course:** The common peroneal nerve wraps around the neck of the fibula and divides into the superficial and deep peroneal nerves. The deep peroneal nerve then passes into the anterior compartment of the leg, running deep to the extensor digitorum longus muscle. It travels alongside the anterior tibial artery and vein.
- **Vulnerability:** The deep peroneal nerve is vulnerable to injury where the common peroneal nerve wraps around the fibular neck (e.g., direct blow, fracture) and as it passes through the interosseous membrane into the anterior compartment. Distal tibial fractures, especially those involving the fibular styloid, can directly injure or entrap the deep peroneal nerve. Casting can also compress the nerve, particularly if too tight.
- **Sensory Distribution:** The deep peroneal nerve provides sensory innervation to the skin of the web space between the first and second toes.
- **Clinical Correlation:** The patient's symptoms (foot drop, weakness of tibialis anterior and extensor hallucis longus, sensory loss in the first web space) are classic signs of deep peroneal nerve injury. The location of the fracture (distal tibia) is a common site for this injury.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Tibialis Posterior muscle:** This muscle is located in the posterior compartment of the leg and is innervated by the tibial nerve. It is responsible for plantarflexion and inversion of the foot. Injury would cause difficulty with heel raises and inversion, not foot drop or sensory loss on the dorsum/first web space.
- (B) **Sural nerve:** This is a purely sensory nerve formed by branches of the tibial and common peroneal nerves. It provides sensation to the posterolateral aspect of the lower leg and the lateral aspect of the foot and ankle. Injury would cause sensory loss in this distribution, not motor weakness or sensory loss in the first web space.
- (C) **Anterior Tibial Artery:** This artery runs within the anterior compartment, supplying the muscles there. While a fracture could potentially injure it, injury would typically present with signs of ischemia (pain, pallor, pulselessness, paresthesia, paralysis - the 5 Ps), not the specific pattern of motor weakness (foot drop) and sensory loss (first web space) seen here. The patient's presentation is primarily neurological.
- (E) **Saphenous nerve:** This is a branch of the femoral nerve that provides sensory innervation to the medial aspect of the leg and foot. It runs superficially in the leg, often accompanying the great saphenous vein. Injury would cause sensory loss on the medial leg/foot, not foot drop or sensory loss on the dorsum/first web space.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Structure                 | Location                  | Innervation (Motor) | Sensory Distribution                               | Primary Action(s)                                     | USMLE Relevance                                                                                                |
| :------------------------ | :------------------------ | :------------------ | :------------------------------------------------- | :----------------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| **Deep Peroneal (Fibular) Nerve** | Anterior Leg Compartment | None (Sensory only) | Web space between 1st & 2nd toes, dorsum of foot | Innervates anterior compartment muscles (dorsiflexion) | Common site of injury with tibial fractures, causes foot drop and sensory loss in 1st web space.                 |
| Tibialis Posterior Muscle | Posterior Leg Compartment | Tibial Nerve        | Sole of foot (medial)                               | Plantarflexion, inversion                               | Innervated by tibial nerve; injury causes difficulty with heel raises and inversion.                             |
| Sural Nerve               | Superficial, Posterior Leg | None (Sensory only) | Posterolateral leg, lateral foot/ankle              | Sensory only                                           | Purely sensory; injury causes sensory loss in its distribution.                                                |
| Anterior Tibial Artery    | Anterior Leg Compartment | None                | None                                               | Supplies anterior compartment muscles                   | Vascular supply; injury causes ischemia, not specific neurological deficits like foot drop and 1st web space numbness. |
| Saphenous Nerve           | Medial Leg                | Femoral Nerve       | Medial leg, medial foot                             | Sensory only                                           | Branch of femoral nerve; injury causes sensory loss on the medial leg/foot.                                     |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The deep peroneal (fibular) nerve innervates the muscles of the anterior compartment of the leg (tibialis anterior, extensor hallucis longus, extensor digitorum longus, fibularis tertius) and provides sensation to the web space between the first and second toes. Injury to this nerve, often associated with fractures around the fibular neck or distal tibia, results in foot drop and sensory loss in its specific distribution. Differentiating this from tibial nerve injury (posterior compartment weakness, plantarflexion weakness, sensory loss on sole) or common peroneal nerve injury (foot drop, lateral leg/dorsum sensory loss) is crucial.

<a id='question-089'></a>

### Question 089: Anterior Leg Innervation: Deep Fibular (Peroneal) Nerve (L4-S1)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The deep fibular (peroneal) nerve innervates the anterior compartment muscles of the leg and provides sensory innervation to the web space between the first and second toes. Injury to this nerve, often occurring near the fibular neck, results in foot drop (inability to dorsiflex the foot) and sensory loss in the specified dermatome. (胫神经深支支配小腿前间室肌肉，并提供第一趾和第二趾之间的网状区域的本体感觉。该神经损伤，通常发生在腓骨颈附近，会导致足下垂（无法背屈足部）和指定皮肤区域的感觉丧失。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male presents to the emergency department after sustaining a closed fracture of the proximal fibula during a fall at home. Radiographs confirm a transverse fracture at the fibular neck. Initial examination reveals intact knee and ankle range of motion, but the patient reports immediate onset of numbness and tingling in the dorsum of his left foot and the area between his first and second toes. On physical examination, he is unable to dorsiflex his left foot against gravity, and his toes exhibit marked plantar flexion. The anterior tibialis muscle is weak (0/5 strength), while the gastrocnemius and soleus muscles (posterior compartment) demonstrate normal strength (5/5). The posterior tibial pulse is palpable, and capillary refill is normal.

Which of the following structures is most likely injured in this patient, leading to his clinical presentation?

(A) Tibial nerve
(B) Sural nerve
(C) Superficial fibular (peroneal) nerve
(D) Deep fibular (peroneal) nerve
(E) Common fibular (peroneal) nerve

#### Correct Answer: (D)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 62-year-old male with a closed fracture of the proximal fibula (fibular neck). This location is critical as it's where the common fibular nerve divides.
- **Symptoms:** Numbness/tingling on the dorsum of the foot and between the first and second toes. This points to sensory involvement in the deep fibular nerve distribution.
- **Motor Deficit:** Inability to dorsiflex the foot (foot drop), weakness of the anterior tibialis muscle (0/5). This indicates motor involvement of the anterior compartment muscles, innervated by the deep fibular nerve.
- **Negative Findings:** Intact posterior compartment function (gastrocnemius/soleus strength 5/5) rules out significant tibial nerve injury. Palpable posterior tibial pulse rules out major vascular compromise.
- **Conclusion:** The combination of foot drop (anterior compartment motor weakness) and sensory loss in the first web space strongly implicates injury to the deep fibular nerve, which runs close to the fibular neck.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Deep Fibular (Peroneal) Nerve:** A branch of the common fibular nerve (L4-S1). It arises typically near the fibular neck, passes deep to the extensor retinaculum (superior and inferior bands) into the anterior compartment of the leg.
- **Innervation:**
    - **Motor:** Innervates all muscles of the anterior compartment: Tibialis anterior (dorsiflexion, inversion), Extensor hallucis longus (dorsiflexion of great toe, inversion), Extensor digitorum longus (dorsiflexion of lateral four toes, eversion), Fibularis (Peroneus) tertius (dorsiflexion, eversion). Injury leads to foot drop (inability to dorsiflex).
    - **Sensory:** Provides sensory innervation to the skin of the web space between the first and second toes (dorsal aspect).
- **Clinical Correlation:** Fracture of the fibular neck is a common cause of deep fibular nerve injury due to its close proximity. The resulting foot drop and specific sensory loss are classic signs.
- **Embryology:** The common fibular nerve, and subsequently the deep fibular nerve, arises from the sciatic nerve (L4-S3), which originates from the lumbosacral plexus.
- **Anatomical Relationships:** The deep fibular nerve passes anterior to the fibular head, then deep to the extensor retinaculum. The superficial fibular nerve passes laterally around the fibular neck. The tibial nerve passes posterior to the medial malleolus.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Tibial nerve:** Innervates the posterior compartment muscles (gastrocnemius, soleus, plantaris, popliteus, tibialis posterior, flexor digitorum longus, flexor hallucis longus) and provides sensory innervation to the sole of the foot. Injury would cause inability to plantarflex or invert the foot, and sensory loss on the sole. This patient has intact posterior compartment function, ruling out tibial nerve injury.
- (B) **Sural nerve:** A cutaneous nerve formed by branches of the tibial and common fibular nerves. It provides sensory innervation to the posterolateral aspect of the lower leg and the lateral aspect of the foot and ankle. Injury would cause sensory loss in this distribution, not foot drop or sensory loss in the first web space.
- (C) **Superficial fibular (peroneal) nerve:** Innervates the lateral compartment muscles (fibularis longus and brevis) and provides sensory innervation to the anterolateral aspect of the lower leg and the dorsum of the foot (excluding the first web space). Injury would cause inability to evert the foot and sensory loss over the anterolateral leg and dorsum of the foot, but not foot drop or first web space sensory loss.
- (E) **Common fibular (peroneal) nerve:** This nerve divides into the superficial and deep fibular nerves, typically near the fibular neck. Injury to the common fibular nerve *before* it divides would cause deficits of *both* the superficial (lateral compartment weakness, eversion deficit) and deep (anterior compartment weakness, foot drop, first web space sensory loss) branches. While possible, the specific symptoms described (foot drop + first web space sensory loss, *without* eversion deficit mentioned) are more precisely localized to the deep fibular nerve itself, which is often injured *after* the common fibular nerve has divided, or specifically at the point of division near the fibular neck. Given the options, the deep fibular nerve is the most specific and likely structure injured based on the presented motor and sensory deficits.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):

| Structure                     | Motor Innervation                                  | Sensory Innervation                                   | Location of Injury (Common Site) | Clinical Presentation                                                                 |
| :---------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------ | :--------------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------ | :------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ |
| **Deep Fibular (Peroneal) N.** | Anterior compartment (Tibialis ant., EDL, EHL, F. tertius) | Web space between 1st and 2nd toes (dorsal)         | Fibular neck/Extensor retinaculum | Foot drop, inability to dorsiflex, sensory loss in 1st web space.                     |
| Superficial Fibular (Peroneal) N. | Lateral compartment (F. longus, F. brevis)       | Anterolateral leg, dorsum of foot (excluding 1st web) | Fibular neck                    | Inability to evert foot, sensory loss over anterolateral leg/dorsum of foot.          |
| Tibial Nerve                  | Posterior compartment (Gastrocnemius, Soleus, etc.) | Sole of foot                                         | Popliteal fossa, tarsal tunnel  | Inability to plantarflex/invert, sensory loss on sole.                               |
| Sural Nerve                   | None (Sensory only)                               | Posterolateral leg, lateral foot/ankle                | Variable                        | Sensory loss in posterolateral leg/lateral foot.                                      |
| Common Fibular (Peroneal) N.  | Both Anterior & Lateral compartments               | Anterolateral leg, dorsum of foot (excluding 1st web) | Fibular neck/head               | Foot drop, inability to evert, sensory loss over anterolateral leg/dorsum of foot. |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The deep fibular nerve is vulnerable to injury near the fibular neck, often due to fractures. It innervates the anterior compartment muscles, causing foot drop upon injury, and provides sensation to the first web space. Differentiating it from the superficial fibular nerve (lateral compartment, different sensory area) and the tibial nerve (posterior compartment, sole sensation) is crucial. Remember the mnemonic "DEEP" for Deep Fibular nerve: **D**orsiflexion, **E**version (via EHL/EDL), **E**xtension of toes, **P**aresthesia in the 1st web space.

<a id='question-090'></a>

### Question 090: Anterior Leg Blood Supply: Anterior Tibial Artery (Transitions to Dorsalis Pedis Artery at Ankle Joint)
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The anterior tibial artery transitions into the dorsalis pedis artery as it passes through the ankle joint, specifically distal to the interosseous membrane and proximal to the extensor retinaculum. 胫前动脉在踝关节处过渡为背动脉，具体位置在骨间膜远端和屈肌滞带近端。

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male with a history of poorly controlled type 2 diabetes mellitus and peripheral vascular disease presents to the emergency department with acute onset of severe pain, pallor, paresthesia, pulselessness, paralysis, and poikilothermia (the "6 Ps") in his right lower leg. The symptoms began approximately 4 hours ago. Physical examination reveals a cool, pale right lower leg with absent dorsalis pedis and posterior tibial pulses. Capillary refill is prolonged (>3 seconds). Neurological examination shows weakness in dorsiflexion of the foot and toes (foot drop) and decreased sensation over the dorsum of the foot and the first three web spaces. Ankle-brachial index (ABI) on the right is 0.4. Emergency angiography confirms complete occlusion of the right anterior tibial artery just distal to the knee joint, with no evidence of distal runoff. The patient is taken emergently to the operating room for vascular surgery. During the procedure, the surgeon identifies the anterior tibial artery proximal to the occlusion. Which of the following structures is immediately distal to the point where the anterior tibial artery typically transitions into the dorsalis pedis artery?

(A) Extensor retinaculum
(B) Interosseous membrane
(C) Tibialis anterior muscle
(D) Peroneus longus muscle
(E) Flexor hallucis longus muscle

#### Correct Answer: (A)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 62-year-old male, diabetes, PVD, acute limb ischemia (6 Ps) in the right lower leg. This points to an acute arterial occlusion.
- **Physical Exam:** Cool, pale leg, absent pulses (dorsalis pedis and posterior tibial), prolonged capillary refill, foot drop (weak dorsiflexion), sensory loss on dorsum of foot (first three web spaces). These findings localize the ischemia to the anterior tibial artery territory and confirm significant arterial compromise.
- **ABI:** 0.4 indicates severe peripheral arterial disease.
- **Angiography:** Confirms occlusion of the right anterior tibial artery distal to the knee.
- **Surgical Context:** The surgeon identifies the anterior tibial artery proximal to the occlusion.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the structure immediately distal to the typical transition point of the anterior tibial artery to the dorsalis pedis artery. This requires knowledge of the anatomical course of the anterior tibial artery in the lower leg and ankle.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- The anterior tibial artery is a direct continuation of the popliteal artery, passing through the interosseous membrane into the anterior compartment of the leg.
- It runs deep to the tibialis anterior muscle, accompanied by the deep fibular (peroneal) nerve.
- As the artery approaches the ankle joint, it passes superior to the interosseous membrane and inferior to the extensor retinaculum (also known as the superior extensor retinaculum or transverse tarsal ligament).
- **Crucially, the anterior tibial artery transitions into the dorsalis pedis artery as it passes *through* the ankle joint, specifically *proximal* to the extensor retinaculum.** The extensor retinaculum is a thick band of connective tissue that spans the gap between the medial and lateral malleoli, holding down the tendons of the extensor muscles.
- The dorsalis pedis artery then courses onto the dorsum of the foot, lateral to the extensor hallucis longus tendon.
- The interosseous membrane is located *proximal* to the ankle joint, separating the anterior and posterior compartments of the leg. The artery passes *through* it, but the transition to the dorsalis pedis occurs *distal* to the membrane and *proximal* to the retinaculum.
- The tibialis anterior muscle is in the anterior compartment, and the artery runs deep to it in the upper leg, but the transition point is distal to the muscle belly.
- The peroneus longus muscle is in the lateral compartment, and the flexor hallucis longus is in the deep posterior compartment; neither is directly related to the transition point of the anterior tibial artery.

##### 3. Embryological Origin (胚胎发生)
- The anterior tibial artery, like other major arteries, develops from the dorsal aortae and their branches during embryogenesis. The specific segmental arteries contributing to the anterior tibial artery arise from the dorsal aorta and fuse to form the descending aorta and its branches. The precise embryological origin isn't directly relevant to the anatomical location asked in the question, but understanding the vascular system's development reinforces its complex branching pattern.

##### 4. Clinical Correlation (临床相关性)
- Occlusion of the anterior tibial artery, as in this case, leads to ischemia of the anterior compartment muscles (tibialis anterior, extensor hallucis longus, extensor digitorum longus, peroneus tertius) and the skin/tissues supplied by its branches (dorsalis pedis artery).
- The sensory distribution of the deep fibular nerve (which accompanies the anterior tibial artery) corresponds to the area of sensory loss described (dorsum of foot, first three web spaces).
- The motor distribution of the deep fibular nerve corresponds to the foot drop (weakness of dorsiflexors).
- Knowing the anatomical transition point is crucial for vascular surgeons performing bypass grafts or endovascular interventions.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Extensor retinaculum:** This is the correct answer. The anterior tibial artery passes *proximal* to the extensor retinaculum as it transitions into the dorsalis pedis artery. The artery is located *between* the retinaculum and the interosseous membrane.
- (B) **Interosseous membrane:** The anterior tibial artery passes *through* the interosseous membrane to enter the anterior compartment of the leg. This membrane is located *proximal* to the ankle joint and the transition point. Therefore, the transition occurs *distal* to the interosseous membrane.
- (C) **Tibialis anterior muscle:** The anterior tibial artery runs deep to the tibialis anterior muscle in the upper part of the leg. While it is in close proximity, the transition to the dorsalis pedis occurs more distally, near the ankle joint, not directly related to the muscle belly itself.
- (D) **Peroneus longus muscle:** This muscle is located in the lateral compartment of the leg, far from the course of the anterior tibial artery and its transition point.
- (E) **Flexor hallucis longus muscle:** This muscle is located in the deep posterior compartment of the leg, also far from the course of the anterior tibial artery and its transition point.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Structure                 | Location Relative to Anterior Tibial Artery Transition | Key Feature                                                                 | Relevance to Question                                                                                                                               |
| :------------------------ | :--------------------------------------------------- | :-------------------------------------------------------------------------- | :-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| **Extensor Retinaculum**  | **Proximal** (Artery passes *under* it)              | Thick band spanning malleoli, holds down extensor tendons.                 | **Correct Answer:** The transition occurs *proximal* to this structure.                                                                               |
| Interosseous Membrane     | Proximal (Artery passes *through* it)                | Separates anterior/posterior leg compartments.                             | Incorrect: Artery passes *through* it *before* reaching the ankle/transition point.                                                                  |
| Tibialis Anterior Muscle  | Proximal/Lateral (Artery runs deep to it)            | Major dorsiflexor of the foot.                                             | Incorrect: Artery is deep to the muscle belly, but the transition point is distal to the muscle.                                                        |
| Peroneus Longus Muscle    | Lateral Compartment (Far from artery)                | Evertor of the foot.                                                        | Incorrect: Located in a different compartment.                                                                                                       |
| Flexor Hallucis Longus Muscle | Deep Posterior Compartment (Far from artery)         | Flexor of the great toe.                                                   | Incorrect: Located in a different compartment.                                                                                                       |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The anterior tibial artery transitions into the dorsalis pedis artery as it passes through the ankle joint, specifically *proximal* to the extensor retinaculum and *distal* to the interosseous membrane. This anatomical relationship is crucial for understanding the blood supply to the foot and ankle and is relevant in cases of vascular injury or disease. Key differentials include confusing the transition point with the interosseous membrane (which is proximal) or the structures within the anterior compartment (like the tibialis anterior muscle, which is also proximal).

<a id='question-091'></a>

### Question 091: Dorsalis Pedis Pulse Palpation
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The anatomical location of the dorsalis pedis artery and pulse, specifically its relationship to the tendons of the extensor compartment of the leg (胫骨前间隙的胫骨后动静脉及其与胫骨前长屈肌和趾长屈肌腱的关系).

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male presents to the emergency department complaining of acute onset right lower extremity pain and numbness after undergoing a percutaneous endoscopic discectomy (PED) for lumbar radiculopathy. During the procedure, the patient was prone for an extended period. Postoperatively, the patient reports significant pain in the dorsum of his right foot, exacerbated by dorsiflexion. Physical examination reveals a diminished dorsalis pedis pulse on the right side compared to the left. Palpation of the dorsum of the right foot reveals tenderness between the tendons of the extensor hallucis longus and extensor digitorum longus muscles. The patient also exhibits sensory loss over the dorsum of the foot and the first web space. Which of the following structures is most likely compromised, leading to the diminished pulse and associated symptoms?

(A) Posterior tibial artery
(B) Peroneal artery
(C) Dorsalis pedis artery
(D) Sural nerve
(E) Saphenous nerve

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 62-year-old male, acute right lower extremity pain/numbness post-lumbar PED, prolonged prone position. This suggests potential compression neuropathy or vascular compromise related to positioning.
- **Symptoms:** Pain in the dorsum of the foot, exacerbated by dorsiflexion. This points towards involvement of structures on the anterior/dorsal aspect of the foot/ankle, potentially related to the extensor compartment.
- **Physical Exam:** Diminished right dorsalis pedis pulse. This directly implicates the dorsalis pedis artery.
- **Palpation:** Tenderness between the tendons of the extensor hallucis longus (EHL) and extensor digitorum longus (EDL). This is the classic anatomical location where the dorsalis pedis artery is palpated.
- **Sensory Loss:** Dorsum of the foot and first web space. This sensory distribution corresponds to the superficial peroneal nerve (also known as the superficial fibular nerve), which runs alongside the dorsalis pedis artery in the extensor compartment.
- **Question Stem:** Asks which structure is *most likely compromised*, leading to the diminished pulse and associated symptoms. The diminished pulse and tenderness location strongly point to the dorsalis pedis artery itself. The sensory loss suggests involvement of the superficial peroneal nerve, which often runs close to the artery and can be affected by the same compressive force or injury. However, the primary finding directly asked about (diminished pulse, tenderness location) is related to the artery.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Dorsalis Pedis Artery:** A continuation of the anterior tibial artery, it passes between the extensor hallucis longus (EHL) medially and the extensor digitorum longus (EDL) laterally on the dorsum of the foot, just proximal to the ankle joint. This is the standard location for palpating the pulse.
- **Extensor Compartment:** Located anteriorly in the leg and dorsally in the foot. Contains muscles responsible for dorsiflexion and toe extension (e.g., tibialis anterior, EHL, EDL, peroneus tertius).
- **Superficial Peroneal Nerve (Superficial Fibular Nerve):** A branch of the common peroneal nerve. It runs superficially in the lateral compartment of the leg, then crosses anterior to the ankle joint, running between the EHL and EDL muscles on the dorsum of the foot, alongside the dorsalis pedis artery. It provides sensory innervation to the anterolateral aspect of the lower leg and the dorsum of the foot (excluding the first web space, which is innervated by the deep peroneal nerve).
- **Clinical Correlation:** Prolonged prone positioning, especially with pressure points, can compress structures against bony prominences. In this case, pressure on the anterior aspect of the ankle/foot could compress the dorsalis pedis artery between the EHL and EDL tendons, leading to a diminished pulse and potentially ischemia. The superficial peroneal nerve, running alongside the artery, could also be compressed, explaining the sensory loss. The tenderness location is the key anatomical landmark for the artery. The question asks which structure is compromised leading to the *diminished pulse*.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Posterior tibial artery:** Located deep in the posterior compartment of the leg, passing behind the medial malleolus. It gives rise to the dorsalis pedis and lateral plantar arteries. It is not palpable on the dorsum of the foot and is not located between the EHL and EDL tendons. Compression here would not cause the described symptoms or pulse finding.
- (B) **Peroneal artery (Fibular artery):** Runs in the posterior compartment of the leg, deep to the soleus muscle. It does not contribute to the dorsalis pedis pulse and is not located on the dorsum of the foot.
- (D) **Sural nerve:** Formed by branches of the tibial and common peroneal nerves. It runs superficially in the posterior aspect of the leg, then passes behind the lateral malleolus and runs distally on the posterolateral aspect of the foot. It provides sensory innervation to the posterolateral aspect of the lower leg and the lateral aspect of the foot. It is not located between the EHL and EDL tendons and is not directly related to the dorsalis pedis pulse.
- (E) **Saphenous nerve:** A branch of the femoral nerve. It runs along the medial aspect of the leg and foot, accompanying the great saphenous vein. It provides sensory innervation to the medial aspect of the lower leg and foot. It is not located on the dorsum of the foot between the EHL and EDL tendons and is not related to the dorsalis pedis pulse.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Structure             | Location                                                                 | Relationship to EHL/EDL | Pulse Association | Sensory Innervation (Foot)                                  | Clinical Relevance (Vignette)                                                                 |
|----------------------|--------------------------------------------------------------------------|--------------------------|-------------------|-------------------------------------------------------------|----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| Dorsalis Pedis Artery | Dorsum of foot, between EHL (medial) and EDL (lateral), proximal to ankle | Directly between         | Yes (palpable)   | None (motor to foot muscles)                               | Diminished pulse, tenderness location matches; likely compressed by prolonged prone position. |
| Posterior Tibial Artery| Deep posterior compartment, behind medial malleolus                      | N/A                      | No                | None (motor to foot muscles)                               | Not relevant to dorsum of foot findings.                                                      |
| Peroneal (Fibular) Artery| Deep posterior compartment                                                | N/A                      | No                | None (motor to foot muscles)                               | Not relevant to dorsum of foot findings.                                                      |
| Sural Nerve           | Posterolateral aspect of leg and foot                                      | N/A                      | No                | Lateral foot                                                | Not relevant to dorsum of foot findings.                                                      |
| Saphenous Nerve       | Medial aspect of leg and foot                                              | N/A                      | No                | Medial foot                                                 | Not relevant to dorsum of foot findings.                                                      |
| Superficial Peroneal Nerve | Runs between EHL/EDL alongside Dorsalis Pedis Artery on dorsum of foot | Alongside                | No                | Anterolateral leg, dorsum of foot (except 1st web space) | Sensory loss matches distribution; often affected with artery compression.                     |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The dorsalis pedis artery is located on the dorsum of the foot, between the extensor hallucis longus (medial) and extensor digitorum longus (lateral) tendons. This is the classic site for palpating the pulse. Compression in this area, often due to prolonged pressure (e.g., prone positioning), can diminish or obliterate the pulse. The superficial peroneal nerve runs alongside the artery in this region and can also be affected by compression, leading to sensory deficits. Remember the key anatomical relationships in the extensor compartment of the foot. Differentiate the dorsalis pedis artery from the posterior tibial artery (deep posterior compartment) and the peroneal artery (deep posterior compartment).

<a id='question-092'></a>

### Question 092: Deep Fibular Nerve Injury & Foot Function
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The deep fibular (peroneal) nerve innervates the anterior compartment muscles of the leg (responsible for dorsiflexion of the foot and extension of the toes) and provides sensory innervation to the web space between the first and second toes. Injury to this nerve results in impaired dorsiflexion, toe extension, and loss of sensation in this specific web space. (深腓神经支配小腿前间室的肌肉（负责足背屈和趾伸展），并为第一趾和第二趾之间的网状间提供感觉。该神经损伤会导致足背屈、趾伸展受损以及该特定网状间的感觉丧失。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male presents to the emergency department after sustaining a closed fracture of the distal fibula during a fall. Initial assessment reveals intact sensation over the dorsum of the foot, except for the web space between the first and second toes. Motor examination demonstrates an inability to dorsiflex the foot or extend the great toe against resistance, while extension of the other toes is possible but weak. Ankle reflexes are normal. Radiographs confirm a displaced fracture of the distal third of the fibula.

Which of the following structures is most likely injured, leading to the patient's specific neurological deficits?

(A) Tibial nerve
(B) Superficial fibular (peroneal) nerve
(C) Sural nerve
(D) Saphenous nerve
(E) Deep fibular (peroneal) nerve

#### Correct Answer: E

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 62-year-old male with a distal fibula fracture after a fall. This immediately points towards potential injury to structures passing near the fibula, particularly the common fibular (peroneal) nerve, which wraps around the fibular neck.
- **Sensory Deficit:** Intact sensation over the dorsum of the foot *except* for the web space between the first and second toes. This specific sensory loss is a hallmark of deep fibular nerve injury.
- **Motor Deficit:** Inability to dorsiflex the foot (involving tibialis anterior, extensor hallucis longus, extensor digitorum longus) and extend the great toe (extensor hallucis longus). These muscles are innervated by the deep fibular nerve. Weakness in extending other toes (extensor digitorum longus) is also consistent.
- **Ankle Reflexes:** Normal ankle reflexes (Achilles reflex) indicate the tibial nerve (S1-S2) is likely intact.
- **Fracture Location:** Distal fibula fracture is a common cause of common fibular nerve injury, which then branches into superficial and deep fibular nerves. The deep fibular nerve is particularly vulnerable as it passes through the anterior compartment.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Deep Fibular Nerve (深腓神经):** A branch of the common fibular (peroneal) nerve (L4-S2). It arises in the anterior aspect of the proximal leg, typically near the fibular neck, after the common fibular nerve divides. It passes deep to the extensor retinaculum (superior and inferior bands) into the anterior compartment of the leg.
- **Innervation (支配):**
    - **Motor (运动):** Innervates all muscles in the anterior compartment of the leg: Tibialis anterior (TA), Extensor hallucis longus (EHL), Extensor digitorum longus (EDL), and Fibularis (Peroneus) tertius (PT). These muscles are responsible for dorsiflexion of the foot (TA, PT), extension of the great toe (EHL), and extension of the lateral four toes (EDL).
    - **Sensory (感觉):** Provides sensory innervation to the skin of the web space between the first and second toes (1st intermetatarsal space).
- **Clinical Correlation (临床相关性):** Injury to the deep fibular nerve, often due to fractures of the fibular neck or head, or compression, results in "foot drop" (inability to dorsiflex the foot) and weakness/inability to extend the great toe. The characteristic sensory loss is confined to the first web space. The patient's presentation perfectly matches this pattern.
- **Embryology (胚胎发生):** The fibular (peroneal) nerves, including the deep branch, arise from the ventral rami of spinal nerves L4, L5, S1, and S2.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Tibial nerve (胫神经):** The terminal branch of the sciatic nerve (L4-S3). It passes through the popliteal fossa and enters the posterior compartment of the leg. It innervates the posterior compartment muscles (plantarflexors, toe flexors) and provides sensory innervation to the sole of the foot. Injury would cause inability to plantarflex or invert the foot, loss of sensation on the sole, and a diminished or absent Achilles reflex. This does not match the patient's presentation.
- (B) **Superficial fibular (peroneal) nerve (浅腓神经):** The other major branch of the common fibular nerve. It innervates the lateral compartment muscles (fibularis longus and brevis), responsible for eversion of the foot. It provides sensory innervation to the dorsum of the foot *except* for the first web space. Injury would cause inability to evert the foot and sensory loss over the dorsum of the foot (excluding the 1st web space). This does not match the patient's motor or sensory deficits.
- (C) **Sural nerve (跟外神经):** Formed by branches of the tibial and common fibular nerves. It provides sensory innervation to the posterolateral aspect of the lower leg and the lateral aspect of the foot and ankle. Injury would cause sensory loss in its distribution, not motor deficits or the specific 1st web space sensory loss.
- (D) **Saphenous nerve (隐静脉神经):** A branch of the femoral nerve (L2-L4). It runs down the medial side of the leg, accompanying the great saphenous vein. It provides sensory innervation to the medial aspect of the leg and foot. Injury would cause sensory loss in this distribution, not the motor deficits or the specific 1st web space sensory loss seen here.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Feature                 | Deep Fibular Nerve (E)                                  | Superficial Fibular Nerve (B)                             | Tibial Nerve (A)                                        | Sural Nerve (C)                                      | Saphenous Nerve (D)                                  |
| :---------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------ | :-------------------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------ | :---------------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------ |
| **Origin**              | Common Fibular Nerve (L4-S2)                            | Common Fibular Nerve (L4-S2)                              | Sciatic Nerve (L4-S3)                                   | Tibial & Common Fibular Nerves                        | Femoral Nerve (L2-L4)                                  |
| **Course**              | Anterior compartment of leg, deep to extensor retinaculum | Lateral compartment of leg, superficial to extensor retinaculum | Posterior compartment of leg, popliteal fossa           | Posterolateral leg, lateral foot/ankle                  | Medial leg, accompanies great saphenous vein            |
| **Motor Innervation**   | Anterior compartment (TA, EHL, EDL, PT)                 | Lateral compartment (FL, FB)                               | Posterior compartment (plantarflexors, toe flexors)     | None                                                  | None                                                  |
| **Sensory Innervation** | 1st web space (between 1st & 2nd toes)                  | Dorsum of foot (excluding 1st web space)                  | Sole of foot                                            | Posterolateral leg, lateral foot/ankle                  | Medial leg, medial foot                                 |
| **Key Deficit**         | Foot drop, weak toe extension, 1st web space sensory loss | Foot eversion weakness, dorsum sensory loss (excl. 1st web) | Plantarflexion weakness, sole sensory loss, ↓Achilles reflex | Sensory loss in distribution                          | Sensory loss in distribution                          |
| **Common Injury Site**  | Fibular neck/head fracture, anterior compartment trauma | Fibular neck/head fracture                               | Popliteal fossa, tarsal tunnel                          | Trauma, compression                                    | Trauma, compression                                    |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The deep fibular nerve innervates the anterior compartment muscles of the leg (dorsiflexors and toe extensors) and provides sensation exclusively to the first web space. Injury typically results from trauma near the fibular neck or head, causing foot drop and specific sensory loss. Differentiating it from the superficial fibular nerve (eversion, dorsum sensation except 1st web space) and the tibial nerve (plantarflexion, sole sensation) is crucial.

<a id='question-093'></a>

### Question 093: Lateral Compartment of the Leg: Fibularis (Peroneus) Longus and Brevis Muscles
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The anatomical relationships, innervation, and potential injury sites of the fibularis (peroneus) longus and brevis muscles within the lateral compartment of the leg, specifically concerning the superficial fibular (peroneal) nerve and its branches. (腿外侧隔间：腓肠肌和腓骨长肌的解剖关系、神经支配及其潜在损伤部位，特别是浅腓神经及其分支。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male presents to the emergency department after sustaining a direct blow to the lateral aspect of his lower leg while playing soccer. He reports immediate, sharp pain and difficulty walking. Physical examination reveals significant tenderness over the fibular head and the lateral malleolus. He exhibits foot eversion weakness (grade 3/5) and sensory loss over the dorsum of the foot and the lateral aspect of the lower leg. Ankle plantarflexion and inversion strength are normal (5/5). The patient denies any history of diabetes or peripheral vascular disease. Radiographs show no acute fracture.

Which of the following structures is most likely injured in this patient?

(A) Tibialis posterior muscle
(B) Deep fibular (peroneal) nerve
(C) Sural nerve
(D) Superficial fibular (peroneal) nerve
(E) Posterior tibial artery

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Mechanism:** Direct blow to the lateral leg. This points towards injury to structures in the lateral compartment or superficial lateral structures.
- **Symptoms:** Immediate pain, difficulty walking, tenderness over fibular head and lateral malleolus. This localizes the injury to the lateral aspect of the lower leg, near the fibula and ankle.
- **Motor Deficit:** Foot eversion weakness (grade 3/5). Eversion is primarily performed by the fibularis (peroneus) longus and brevis muscles, located in the lateral compartment.
- **Sensory Deficit:** Sensory loss over the dorsum of the foot and the lateral aspect of the lower leg. This sensory distribution corresponds to the territory of the superficial fibular (peroneal) nerve.
- **Normal Findings:** Normal ankle plantarflexion (gastrocnemius, soleus) and inversion (tibialis posterior, tibialis anterior) strength rules out significant injury to the posterior or deep anterior compartments, or the tibial nerve.
- **Conclusion:** The combination of lateral leg trauma, eversion weakness, and sensory loss over the dorsum of the foot and lateral leg strongly suggests injury to the fibularis (peroneus) longus and brevis muscles and/or the nerve that innervates them, the superficial fibular (peroneal) nerve. The superficial fibular nerve runs superficially in the lateral compartment, making it vulnerable to direct trauma in this region.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Lateral Compartment Muscles:** The fibularis (peroneus) longus and brevis muscles are located in the lateral compartment of the leg, deep to the fascia but superficial to the fibula. They originate from the upper two-thirds of the fibula and insert onto the base of the 5th metatarsal (longus) and the tuberosity of the 5th metatarsal (brevis). Their primary action is foot eversion.
- **Innervation:** Both muscles are innervated by the superficial fibular (peroneal) nerve, a branch of the common fibular (peroneal) nerve. The common fibular nerve branches into the superficial and deep fibular nerves posterior to the fibular head. The superficial fibular nerve runs superficially in the lateral compartment, passing between the fibularis longus and brevis muscles, before winding anteriorly around the fibular neck to supply these muscles and provide cutaneous innervation.
- **Superficial Fibular Nerve Course & Sensory Distribution:** The superficial fibular nerve provides motor innervation to the fibularis longus and brevis muscles. It also provides sensory innervation to the skin over the anterolateral aspect of the lower leg and the dorsum of the foot (excluding the web space between the 1st and 2nd toes, which is innervated by the deep fibular nerve).
- **Clinical Correlation:** Trauma to the lateral aspect of the leg, particularly near the fibular head and neck, can directly injure the superficial fibular nerve as it courses superficially in the lateral compartment. This injury would result in weakness of the fibularis longus and brevis muscles (leading to eversion weakness) and sensory loss in the distribution of the nerve (lateral lower leg and dorsum of the foot).
- **Embryology:** The fibularis longus and brevis muscles develop from the lateral compartment of the leg, originating from somites. They are innervated by the sciatic nerve (specifically its fibular component) during development, which later differentiates into the common fibular nerve and its branches.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Tibialis posterior muscle:** Located in the deep posterior compartment of the leg. Innervated by the tibial nerve. Primary action is plantarflexion and inversion of the foot. Injury would cause weakness in plantarflexion and inversion, and sensory loss on the sole of the foot (medial aspect). This does not match the patient's presentation (eversion weakness, lateral leg/dorsum foot sensory loss).
- (B) **Deep fibular (peroneal) nerve:** Runs deep within the anterior compartment of the leg, passing through the interosseous membrane. Innervates the anterior compartment muscles (tibialis anterior, extensor hallucis longus, extensor digitorum longus, fibularis tertius) and provides sensory innervation to the web space between the 1st and 2nd toes. Injury would cause foot drop (weak dorsiflexion, toe extension) and sensory loss in that specific web space. This does not match the patient's presentation (eversion weakness, lateral leg/dorsum foot sensory loss).
- (C) **Sural nerve:** A cutaneous nerve formed by branches of the tibial and common fibular nerves. It runs superficially down the posterior aspect of the leg and then laterally around the ankle, providing sensation to the posterolateral aspect of the lower leg and the lateral aspect of the foot and ankle. It does not have a motor function in the leg. Injury would cause sensory loss in its distribution, but not motor weakness of the evertors. This does not match the patient's presentation (eversion weakness).
- (E) **Posterior tibial artery:** The main artery of the posterior compartment of the leg. It runs deep to the muscles. Injury would typically cause signs of vascular compromise (pallor, pulselessness, paresthesia, paralysis, poikilothermia) in the foot, not isolated eversion weakness and specific sensory loss pattern. This does not match the patient's presentation.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Structure                     | Location                                  | Innervation (Motor) | Sensory Distribution                                  | Primary Action(s) | Common Injury Mechanism/Symptoms                                                                                                                               |
| :--------------------------- | :---------------------------------------- | :------------------ | :--------------------------------------------------- | :---------------- | :----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| **Superficial Fibular (Peroneal) Nerve** | Superficial lateral compartment, around fibular neck | Fibularis longus/brevis | Lateral lower leg, dorsum of foot (except 1st web space) | Eversion          | Direct trauma to lateral leg, ankle sprain. Eversion weakness, sensory loss as described.                                                                    |
| Deep Fibular (Peroneal) Nerve | Deep anterior compartment                  | Anterior compartment muscles | Web space between 1st and 2nd toes                     | Dorsiflexion, Toe Extension | Compression (e.g., fracture), compartment syndrome. Foot drop, sensory loss in 1st web space.                                                               |
| Tibial Nerve                 | Deep posterior compartment                  | Posterior compartment muscles | Sole of foot (medial 3.5 toes), posterior leg          | Plantarflexion, Inversion | Trauma to posterior leg/ankle, tarsal tunnel syndrome. Plantarflexion/inversion weakness, sensory loss on sole.                                                |
| Sural Nerve                  | Superficial posterior/lateral leg           | None                | Posterolateral lower leg, lateral foot/ankle           | Sensory only      | Trauma, skin grafts. Sensory loss in distribution.                                                                                                           |
| Tibialis Posterior Muscle    | Deep posterior compartment                  | Tibial Nerve        | Sole of foot (medial 3.5 toes)                         | Plantarflexion, Inversion | Tendon rupture (often atraumatic), trauma. Difficulty with inversion, flatfoot.                                                                               |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The superficial fibular (peroneal) nerve is vulnerable to injury due to its superficial course around the fibular neck in the lateral compartment of the leg. Injury typically results from direct trauma. Clinically, this presents with weakness of foot eversion (fibularis longus and brevis muscles) and sensory loss over the lateral lower leg and dorsum of the foot. Differentiating it from deep fibular nerve injury (foot drop, 1st web space sensory loss) and tibial nerve injury (plantarflexion/inversion weakness, sole sensory loss) is crucial.
