<a id='question-094'></a>

### Question 094: Lateral Leg Innervation
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The superficial fibular (peroneal) nerve (L5-S1) innervates the muscles of the lateral compartment of the leg (fibularis longus and brevis) and provides sensory innervation to the dorsum of the foot and the anterolateral aspect of the lower leg. Injury to this nerve, often at the fibular head, results in foot drop (inability to dorsiflex and evert the foot) and sensory loss in its distribution. (浅腓神经 (L5-S1) 支配小腿外侧区肌肉 (腓肠肌和腓骨长肌) 并为足背和下腿前外侧提供感觉。该神经损伤，通常发生在腓骨头处，会导致足下垂 (无法背屈和外翻足) 和感觉丧失在其分布区域。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 45-year-old male construction worker presents to the emergency department after tripping over a piece of rebar at a construction site. He reports immediate, severe pain in his right lower leg. On examination, he is unable to stand on his toes or heel on the affected side. His ankle is in a neutral position, and he cannot actively dorsiflex his foot against resistance. Palpation reveals tenderness over the lateral aspect of the proximal fibula. Sensory examination reveals decreased sensation over the dorsum of his right foot and the anterolateral aspect of his lower leg. Distal pulses (dorsalis pedis and posterior tibial) are palpable and strong. Ankle reflexes (Achilles) are intact bilaterally.

Which of the following structures is most likely injured?

(A) Tibial nerve
(B) Deep fibular (peroneal) nerve
(C) Sural nerve
(D) Common fibular (peroneal) nerve
(E) Saphenous nerve

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Mechanism:** Tripping over rebar suggests a direct blow or traction injury to the lateral aspect of the proximal lower leg.
- **Symptoms:** Immediate severe pain in the right lower leg.
- **Motor Deficits:** Inability to stand on toes (indicates weakness of gastrocnemius/soleus, innervated by tibial nerve) and inability to stand on heel (indicates weakness of tibialis anterior, innervated by deep fibular nerve) and inability to dorsiflex the foot (tibialis anterior, extensor hallucis longus, extensor digitorum longus - innervated by deep fibular nerve). Inability to evert the foot (fibularis longus and brevis - innervated by superficial fibular nerve). The description "cannot actively dorsiflex his foot against resistance" points strongly towards deep fibular nerve involvement, but the inability to stand on toes suggests a more proximal injury affecting the common fibular nerve before its bifurcation, or potentially both branches. However, the sensory loss pattern is key.
- **Sensory Deficits:** Decreased sensation over the dorsum of the foot and anterolateral aspect of the lower leg. This is the classic distribution of the superficial fibular nerve.
- **Physical Exam:** Tenderness over the lateral aspect of the proximal fibula, the common site for common fibular nerve injury. Intact distal pulses rule out major vascular injury. Intact Achilles reflex suggests the tibial nerve is likely spared (though a proximal common fibular nerve injury *can* sometimes affect the tibial nerve indirectly or the reflex arc).
- **Synthesis:** The combination of pain over the fibular head, inability to dorsiflex and evert the foot (motor), and sensory loss over the dorsum of the foot and anterolateral leg strongly points to injury of the common fibular nerve, which branches into the superficial and deep fibular nerves. The superficial fibular nerve is primarily responsible for the sensory loss described and innervation of the lateral compartment muscles (evertors). The deep fibular nerve innervates the anterior compartment muscles (dorsiflexors) and provides sensation to the web space between the first and second toes. The injury described likely affects the common fibular nerve, or at least the superficial branch, given the prominent sensory loss in its distribution. Since the common fibular nerve is the parent nerve that gives rise to both superficial and deep branches, and the injury site is at the fibular head, injury to the common fibular nerve is the most likely diagnosis encompassing the observed deficits.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Common Fibular (Peroneal) Nerve:** Arises from the sciatic nerve in the posterior thigh. It winds laterally around the posterior aspect of the proximal tibia, passing superficial to the lateral head of the gastrocnemius muscle. It then descends into the lateral compartment of the leg, passing between the two heads of the fibularis longus muscle, deep to the fibular head. At the fibular neck, it divides into the superficial and deep fibular nerves.
- **Superficial Fibular Nerve:** The larger terminal branch. It runs down the lateral compartment, innervating the fibularis longus and brevis muscles (responsible for eversion). It then passes deep to the fibularis longus tendon, through the fibular retinaculum, and onto the dorsum of the foot. It provides sensory innervation to the anterolateral aspect of the lower leg and the dorsum of the foot (except the web space between the first and second toes).
- **Deep Fibular Nerve:** The smaller terminal branch. It runs down the anterior compartment, innervating the tibialis anterior, extensor hallucis longus, extensor digitorum longus, and fibularis tertius muscles (responsible for dorsiflexion and toe extension). It provides sensory innervation to the web space between the first and second toes.
- **Tibial Nerve:** The other major terminal branch of the sciatic nerve. It runs down the posterior compartment of the leg, innervating the posterior compartment muscles (plantarflexors, invertors) and providing sensory innervation to the sole of the foot.
- **Sural Nerve:** Formed by branches of the tibial and common fibular nerves. It runs down the posterior aspect of the leg, providing sensory innervation to the posterolateral aspect of the lower leg and the lateral aspect of the foot.
- **Saphenous Nerve:** A branch of the femoral nerve. It runs down the medial aspect of the leg, providing sensory innervation to the medial aspect of the lower leg and foot.
- **Injury Location:** The fibular head is a common site for common fibular nerve injury due to its superficial course around the bone.
- **Clinical Correlation:** Injury to the common fibular nerve results in weakness of dorsiflexion (deep branch) and eversion (superficial branch), leading to foot drop and inability to stand on the toes or heel. Sensory loss occurs in the distribution of both the superficial and deep branches, although the superficial branch distribution is typically more extensive. The vignette describes sensory loss consistent with the superficial branch and motor deficits consistent with both branches, suggesting an injury proximal to the bifurcation, i.e., the common fibular nerve.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Tibial nerve:** Incorrect. The tibial nerve innervates the posterior compartment muscles (plantarflexion, inversion) and the sole of the foot. Injury would cause inability to stand on toes (due to gastrocnemius/soleus weakness), but *not* inability to dorsiflex or evert the foot, nor sensory loss on the dorsum of the foot or anterolateral leg. The Achilles reflex might also be diminished or absent.
- (B) **Deep fibular (peroneal) nerve:** Incorrect. The deep fibular nerve innervates the anterior compartment muscles (dorsiflexion, toe extension) and provides sensation to the web space between the first and second toes. Injury would cause foot drop (inability to dorsiflex) and inability to extend the great toe, but would *not* cause inability to evert the foot (superficial branch) or sensory loss over the anterolateral leg and most of the dorsum of the foot (superficial branch distribution). While the vignette mentions inability to dorsiflex, the sensory loss pattern strongly points away from an isolated deep fibular nerve injury.
- (C) **Sural nerve:** Incorrect. The sural nerve provides sensory innervation to the posterolateral lower leg and lateral foot. Injury would cause sensory loss in this distribution, not the anterolateral leg or dorsum of the foot. It has no motor function in the leg.
- (E) **Saphenous nerve:** Incorrect. The saphenous nerve provides sensory innervation to the medial lower leg and medial foot. Injury would cause sensory loss in this distribution, not the anterolateral leg or dorsum of the foot. It has no motor function in the leg.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Structure                     | Innervation (Motor)                                  | Innervation (Sensory)                                                                 | Key Clinical Deficit(s)                                                                                                | Location of Injury (Common Site) |
| :---------------------------- | :--------------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | :----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------- |
| **Common Fibular (Peroneal)** | Fibularis longus/brevis (Superficial), Tib Ant, EHL, EDL, Fib Tertius (Deep) | Anterolateral leg, Dorsum foot (except 1st/2nd toe web space) (Superficial), 1st/2nd toe web space (Deep) | Foot drop (dorsiflexion weakness), Inability to evert foot, Sensory loss anterolateral leg & dorsum foot. | Fibular head                    |
| Tibial Nerve                  | Posterior compartment muscles (plantarflexion, inversion) | Sole of foot                                                                          | Inability to stand on toes (plantarflexion weakness), Sensory loss on sole.                                             | Popliteal fossa, Tarsal tunnel   |
| Deep Fibular (Peroneal) Nerve | Tib Ant, EHL, EDL, Fib Tertius                        | Web space between 1st and 2nd toes                                                     | Foot drop (dorsiflexion weakness), Inability to extend great toe.                                                        | Proximal leg                    |
| Superficial Fibular (Peroneal) Nerve | Fibularis longus, Fibularis brevis                  | Anterolateral leg, Dorsum foot (except 1st/2nd toe web space)                             | Inability to evert foot, Sensory loss anterolateral leg & dorsum foot.                                                   | Proximal leg                    |
| Sural Nerve                   | None                                                 | Posterolateral leg, Lateral foot                                                        | Sensory loss posterolateral leg & lateral foot.                                                                          | Posterior leg                    |
| Saphenous Nerve               | None                                                 | Medial leg, Medial foot                                                                 | Sensory loss medial leg & medial foot.                                                                                    | Medial leg                      |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The common fibular (peroneal) nerve is vulnerable to injury at the fibular head, often causing foot drop due to paralysis of the dorsiflexors (deep branch) and everters (superficial branch), along with sensory loss over the anterolateral leg and dorsum of the foot. Differentiating common fibular nerve injury from deep or superficial fibular nerve injury relies heavily on the pattern of motor and sensory deficits. Remember the mnemonic "DEEP" for the deep fibular nerve sensory distribution (Dorsal web space between the first two toes, Extensor digitorum longus, Extensor hallucis longus, Peroneus tertius). The superficial fibular nerve covers the rest of the dorsum and anterolateral leg.

<a id='question-095'></a>

### Question 095: Superficial Fibular Nerve Injury
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The superficial fibular (peroneal) nerve innervates the muscles responsible for foot eversion (peroneus longus and brevis) and provides sensory innervation to the anterolateral aspect of the lower leg and the dorsum of the foot. Injury to this nerve results in impaired eversion and sensory loss in its distribution. (浅腓神经支配负责足外翻的肌肉（腓骨长肌和腓骨短肌），并为小腿外侧和足背提供感觉。该神经损伤会导致外翻受损和感觉丧失在其分布区域。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old male construction worker presents to the emergency department after sustaining an injury to his right lower leg while operating a jackhammer. He reports immediate, sharp pain followed by numbness and weakness. Physical examination reveals an inability to evert the right foot against resistance. Sensory testing demonstrates decreased sensation over the anterolateral aspect of the lower leg, extending to the dorsum of the foot, specifically between the first and second toes. Motor examination shows weakness in the muscles responsible for foot eversion. The dorsalis pedis pulse is palpable, and the posterior tibial pulse is normal. Ankle reflexes (Achilles and patellar) are intact.

Which of the following structures is most likely injured?

(A) Tibial nerve
(B) Deep fibular (peroneal) nerve
(C) Common fibular (peroneal) nerve
(D) Sural nerve
(E) Saphenous nerve

#### Correct Answer: (C)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 32-year-old male construction worker (suggests potential trauma).
- **Mechanism:** Injury to the right lower leg while using a jackhammer (suggests direct trauma or compression).
- **Symptoms:** Immediate sharp pain, numbness, weakness.
- **Motor Deficit:** Inability to evert the right foot against resistance (key finding pointing to muscles innervated by the superficial fibular nerve: peroneus longus and brevis).
- **Sensory Deficit:** Decreased sensation over the anterolateral lower leg and dorsum of the foot, especially between the first and second toes (classic distribution of the superficial fibular nerve).
- **Vascular Status:** Palpable dorsalis pedis and posterior tibial pulses (rules out major vascular compromise).
- **Reflexes:** Intact ankle and patellar reflexes (suggests the injury is distal to the origins of these reflexes, likely involving the fibular nerve or its branches).

The combination of impaired foot eversion (peroneus longus and brevis) and sensory loss over the anterolateral lower leg and dorsum of the foot strongly implicates the superficial fibular nerve or its parent nerve, the common fibular nerve, proximal to the branching point.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Common Fibular (Peroneal) Nerve:** Arises from the sciatic nerve in the posterior thigh, typically at the level of the popliteal fossa. It winds laterally around the neck of the fibula, deep to the fascia, where it is vulnerable to injury (e.g., fracture, compression).
- **Bifurcation:** The common fibular nerve divides into the superficial and deep fibular nerves. This bifurcation typically occurs in the anterior compartment of the leg, near the head of the fibula, or sometimes even higher.
- **Superficial Fibular Nerve:** The more superficial branch. It passes anteriorly around the neck of the fibula, superficial to the extensor retinaculum, and enters the lateral compartment of the leg.
    - **Motor Innervation:** Innervates peroneus longus and peroneus brevis (responsible for foot eversion).
    - **Sensory Innervation:** Provides sensory innervation to the anterolateral aspect of the lower leg and the dorsum of the foot, excluding the web space between the first and second toes (which is innervated by the deep fibular nerve).
- **Deep Fibular Nerve:** The deeper branch. It passes into the anterior compartment of the leg, deep to the extensor retinaculum.
    - **Motor Innervation:** Innervates the anterior compartment muscles (tibialis anterior, extensor hallucis longus, extensor digitorum longus, peroneus tertius).
    - **Sensory Innervation:** Provides sensory innervation to the web space between the first and second toes.
- **Tibial Nerve:** The other major branch of the sciatic nerve. It runs down the posterior compartment of the leg.
    - **Motor Innervation:** Innervates the posterior compartment muscles (gastrocnemius, soleus, plantaris, popliteus, tibialis posterior, flexor hallucis longus, flexor digitorum longus) and intrinsic foot muscles (except the first dorsal interosseous).
    - **Sensory Innervation:** Provides sensory innervation to the sole of the foot and the posterior aspect of the lower leg.
- **Sural Nerve:** Formed by branches of the tibial and common fibular nerves. It runs down the posterior aspect of the leg, superficial to the Achilles tendon, and provides sensory innervation to the posterolateral aspect of the lower leg and the lateral aspect of the foot and heel.
- **Saphenous Nerve:** A branch of the femoral nerve. It runs down the medial aspect of the leg, accompanying the great saphenous vein.
    - **Motor Innervation:** Innervates sartorius (also femoral nerve) and gracilis (also obturator nerve).
    - **Sensory Innervation:** Provides sensory innervation to the medial aspect of the lower leg and the medial side of the foot.

Given the patient's symptoms (impaired eversion + sensory loss over anterolateral leg/dorsum of foot), the injury affects the nerve supplying the peroneus longus and brevis and the corresponding sensory area. This points directly to the superficial fibular nerve or the common fibular nerve proximal to its bifurcation. Since the common fibular nerve gives rise to the superficial fibular nerve, injury to the common fibular nerve would produce these findings.

##### 3. Embryological Origin (胚胎发生)
The common fibular nerve, like all peripheral nerves, originates from the neural crest cells that migrate to form the peripheral nervous system. Specifically, the lumbosacral plexus gives rise to the sciatic nerve, which then branches into the tibial and common fibular nerves. The common fibular nerve itself is derived from spinal nerve roots L4, L5, and S1.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Tibial nerve:** Incorrect. The tibial nerve innervates the posterior compartment muscles and the sole of the foot. Injury would cause weakness in plantarflexion and toe flexion, and sensory loss on the sole. This patient has impaired eversion and sensory loss on the dorsum/anterolateral leg.
- (B) **Deep fibular (peroneal) nerve:** Incorrect. The deep fibular nerve innervates the anterior compartment muscles (dorsiflexors) and the web space between the first and second toes. Injury would cause foot drop (inability to dorsiflex) and sensory loss only in that specific web space. This patient has impaired eversion (lateral compartment) and broader sensory loss.
- (D) **Sural nerve:** Incorrect. The sural nerve provides sensory innervation to the posterolateral lower leg and lateral foot. Injury would cause sensory loss in this distribution, not the anterolateral leg or dorsum of the foot, and would not cause motor weakness related to eversion or dorsiflexion.
- (E) **Saphenous nerve:** Incorrect. The saphenous nerve provides sensory innervation to the medial lower leg and medial foot. Injury would cause sensory loss in this distribution, not the anterolateral leg or dorsum of the foot, and does not cause motor weakness related to eversion or dorsiflexion.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Feature             | Common Fibular Nerve (Injury) | Tibial Nerve (Injury) | Deep Fibular Nerve (Injury) | Sural Nerve (Injury) | Saphenous Nerve (Injury) |
|----------------------|---------------------------------|------------------------|------------------------------|-----------------------|--------------------------|
| **Motor Function**   | Eversion (Peroneus L/B), Dorsiflexion (Anterior Comp) | Plantarflexion, Toe Flexion | Dorsiflexion (Anterior Comp) | None                   | None                    |
| **Sensory Area**     | Anterolateral Leg, Dorsum Foot (excl. 1st/2nd toe web) | Sole of Foot, Posterior Leg | Web Space 1st/2nd Toe     | Posterolateral Leg, Lateral Foot | Medial Leg, Medial Foot |
| **Key Muscles Affected** | Peroneus Longus/Brevis, Tibialis Anterior, EDL, EHL | Gastrocnemius, Soleus, FHL, FDL | Tibialis Anterior, EDL, EHL, Peroneus Tertius | None                   | None                    |
| **Location of Injury** | Neck of Fibula                 | Posterior Leg          | Anterior Leg (deep)          | Posterior Leg (superficial) | Medial Leg               |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The common fibular nerve is vulnerable to injury as it wraps around the neck of the fibula. Injury proximal to its bifurcation into superficial and deep branches results in loss of function of both branches: impaired foot eversion (superficial) and dorsiflexion (deep), along with sensory loss over the anterolateral lower leg and dorsum of the foot (superficial) and the first web space (deep). Differentiating fibular nerve injuries from tibial, sural, and saphenous nerve injuries relies on understanding their distinct motor and sensory distributions.

<a id='question-096'></a>

### Question 096: Common Fibular (Peroneal) Nerve Injury
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The common fibular (peroneal) nerve's superficial course around the fibular neck makes it vulnerable to injury, leading to characteristic sensory and motor deficits in the lower leg and foot. (胫总神经在腓骨颈的浅表走行使其容易受伤，导致小腿和足部特有的感觉和运动功能障碍。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male presents to the emergency department after sustaining a direct blow to the lateral aspect of his right knee during a fall while hiking. He reports immediate, severe pain on the outside of his lower leg. Physical examination reveals an inability to dorsiflex or evert his right foot against resistance. Sensory examination shows decreased sensation over the dorsum of his right foot and the lateral aspect of his lower leg. The patient is able to plantarflex his foot normally. Ankle reflexes are absent on the right side. Radiographs of the knee show no fracture.

Which of the following structures is most likely injured?

(A) Tibial nerve
(B) Sural nerve
(C) Deep fibular (peroneal) nerve
(D) Superficial fibular (peroneal) nerve
(E) Common fibular (peroneal) nerve

#### Correct Answer: E

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Mechanism:** Direct blow to the lateral knee. This points towards injury to structures located laterally, specifically around the fibular head/neck.
- **Symptoms:** Immediate severe pain on the lateral lower leg. This localizes the injury site.
- **Motor Deficits:** Inability to dorsiflex or evert the foot. Dorsiflexion is primarily innervated by the deep fibular (peroneal) nerve (tibialis anterior, extensor hallucis longus, extensor digitorum longus). Eversion is primarily innervated by the superficial fibular (peroneal) nerve (peroneus longus, peroneus brevis). The combination of both deficits indicates injury to a nerve proximal to the branching point of the common fibular nerve.
- **Sensory Deficits:** Decreased sensation over the dorsum of the foot and lateral lower leg. The dorsum of the foot is innervated by the deep fibular nerve. The lateral lower leg is innervated by the superficial fibular nerve. Again, deficits in both territories point to an injury proximal to the bifurcation.
- **Preserved Function:** Normal plantarflexion. Plantarflexion is innervated by the tibial nerve (gastrocnemius, soleus, plantaris). This helps rule out tibial nerve injury.
- **Reflexes:** Absent ankle reflexes (Achilles reflex). The Achilles reflex involves the tibial nerve (sensory) and S1 nerve root (motor via gastrocnemius/soleus). While absent reflexes can occur with common fibular nerve injury due to pain or associated trauma, the primary motor and sensory deficits strongly point to the common fibular nerve.
- **Imaging:** No fracture. Rules out bony injury as the primary cause of the neurological deficits.
- **Conclusion:** The combination of motor deficits (dorsiflexion and eversion) and sensory deficits (dorsum of foot and lateral leg) following trauma to the lateral knee strongly suggests injury to the common fibular (peroneal) nerve at or near the fibular neck, where it is most superficial.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Common Fibular (Peroneal) Nerve:** A branch of the sciatic nerve (L4-S2). It arises in the posterior thigh, descends laterally, and passes posterior to the head of the fibula before winding superficially around the *neck* of the fibula. At the fibular neck, it divides into the superficial and deep fibular nerves.
- **Location at Fibular Neck:** The common fibular nerve is particularly vulnerable here because it lies superficially, just deep to the fascia lata, overlying the bone. Direct trauma, compression (e.g., leg crossing, prolonged squatting), or fractures of the fibular neck can injure it.
- **Innervation:**
    - **Motor (via branches):**
        - *Deep Fibular Nerve:* Dorsiflexors of the foot and toes (tibialis anterior, extensor hallucis longus, extensor digitorum longus, peroneus tertius) and toe extensors.
        - *Superficial Fibular Nerve:* Evertors of the foot (peroneus longus, peroneus brevis).
    - **Sensory:**
        - *Deep Fibular Nerve:* Web space between the 1st and 2nd toes (dorsum).
        - *Superficial Fibular Nerve:* Lateral aspect of the lower leg and dorsum of the foot (excluding the web space between 1st/2nd toes).
- **Clinical Correlation:** The patient's inability to dorsiflex (deep branch) and evert (superficial branch) the foot, combined with sensory loss in both the lateral leg and dorsum of the foot, indicates injury to the common fibular nerve *before* its bifurcation at the fibular neck. This location is consistent with the mechanism of injury (direct blow to the lateral knee).

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Tibial nerve:** The tibial nerve descends posteriorly in the leg, deep to the soleus muscle. It innervates the posterior compartment muscles (plantarflexors, toe flexors) and provides sensation to the sole of the foot. Injury would cause inability to plantarflex, loss of sensation on the sole, and potentially weakness of toe flexion. This is inconsistent with the patient's presentation (preserved plantarflexion, sensory loss on dorsum/lateral leg).
- (B) **Sural nerve:** The sural nerve is a cutaneous nerve formed by branches of the tibial and common fibular nerves. It runs superficially down the posterior aspect of the calf and provides sensation to the posterolateral aspect of the lower leg and the lateral aspect of the foot. Injury would cause sensory loss in its distribution but no motor deficits. This is inconsistent with the patient's motor deficits.
- (C) **Deep fibular (peroneal) nerve:** This nerve is a branch of the common fibular nerve, arising *after* it winds around the fibular neck. Injury to the deep fibular nerve alone would cause weakness of dorsiflexion and sensory loss in the web space between the 1st and 2nd toes, but eversion would be intact, and sensation on the lateral leg would be preserved. This is inconsistent with the patient's full presentation.
- (D) **Superficial fibular (peroneal) nerve:** This nerve is also a branch of the common fibular nerve, arising *after* it winds around the fibular neck. Injury to the superficial fibular nerve alone would cause weakness of eversion and sensory loss on the lateral leg and dorsum of the foot (excluding the 1st web space), but dorsiflexion would be intact. This is inconsistent with the patient's full presentation.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Structure                     | Location of Injury | Motor Deficits                                  | Sensory Deficits                                       | Clinical Presentation                                                                                                |
| :---------------------------- | :----------------- | :---------------------------------------------- | :----------------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Common Fibular (Peroneal) N. | Fibular Neck       | Weakness of dorsiflexion AND eversion           | Loss of sensation on dorsum of foot AND lateral leg     | Foot drop (inability to dorsiflex), inability to evert, sensory loss lateral leg/dorsum foot, absent ankle reflex. |
| Tibial Nerve                  | Posterior Leg      | Weakness of plantarflexion, toe flexion         | Loss of sensation on sole of foot                      | Inability to stand on toes, sensory loss on sole.                                                                    |
| Deep Fibular (Peroneal) N.    | Distal Leg         | Weakness of dorsiflexion                        | Loss of sensation in 1st web space (dorsum)            | Foot drop, sensory loss in 1st web space.                                                                          |
| Superficial Fibular (Peroneal) N. | Distal Leg         | Weakness of eversion                            | Loss of sensation on lateral leg and dorsum (except 1st web) | Inability to evert, sensory loss lateral leg/dorsum foot.                                                              |
| Sural Nerve                   | Posterior Calf     | None                                            | Loss of sensation on posterolateral calf and lateral foot | Sensory loss only.                                                                                                   |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The common fibular (peroneal) nerve is vulnerable to injury as it wraps superficially around the neck of the fibula. Injury at this location results in deficits in both dorsiflexion (deep branch) and eversion (superficial branch), along with sensory loss over the lateral leg and dorsum of the foot. Differentiating common fibular nerve injury from injuries to its branches (deep or superficial) or the tibial nerve requires careful assessment of the specific motor and sensory deficits. Remember the mnemonic "DEEP" for the deep fibular nerve: Dorsiflexion, Extension of toes, Eversion (weak), Proprioception (foot), Sensory (1st web space). Remember the mnemonic "SUPERFICIAL" for the superficial fibular nerve: Sensation (lateral leg/dorsum foot), Upper leg (peroneus longus/brevis), Eversion, Reflex (absent ankle reflex often), Function (peroneus longus/brevis).

<a id='question-097'></a>

### Question 097: Fibular Neck Fracture / Tight Cast Compression: Common Fibular Nerve Transection / Entrapment
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The common fibular (peroneal) nerve's anatomical course around the fibular neck makes it vulnerable to injury from fractures or compression, leading to characteristic sensory and motor deficits in the lower leg and foot. (胫总神经在腓骨颈的解剖位置使其容易受到骨折或压迫的损伤，导致小腿和足部特有的感觉和运动功能障碍。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old male presents to the emergency department after sustaining a closed fracture of the proximal fibula during a skiing accident. Initial radiographs confirm a transverse fracture at the fibular neck. A posterior long-leg cast is applied for immobilization. Three days later, the patient complains of worsening pain and numbness in the lateral aspect of his lower leg and the dorsum of his foot. On physical examination, he exhibits foot drop (inability to dorsiflex the foot against gravity) and diminished sensation over the lateral lower leg and dorsum of the foot. Sensory testing reveals intact sensation on the medial aspect of the lower leg and the plantar surface of the foot. Motor examination shows weakness in ankle eversion.

Which of the following structures is most likely compromised, leading to these findings?

(A) Tibial nerve
(B) Sural nerve
(C) Deep fibular (peroneal) nerve
(D) Superficial fibular (peroneal) nerve
(E) Sciatic nerve

#### Correct Answer: (D)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 32-year-old male, active (skier).
- **Injury:** Closed fracture of the proximal fibula (fibular neck).
- **Intervention:** Posterior long-leg cast.
- **Symptoms:** Worsening pain and numbness in the lateral lower leg and dorsum of the foot after 3 days.
- **Physical Exam Findings:**
    - Foot drop (inability to dorsiflex).
    - Diminished sensation over the lateral lower leg and dorsum of the foot.
    - Intact sensation on the medial lower leg and plantar foot.
    - Weakness in ankle eversion.
- **Key Clues:** The combination of a fibular neck fracture, cast application, foot drop (dorsiflexion weakness), sensory loss over the lateral leg/dorsum of the foot, and eversion weakness strongly points to injury of the common fibular (peroneal) nerve, specifically its branches. The location of the fracture (fibular neck) is where the nerve wraps around the bone, making it susceptible to injury. The cast can exacerbate the injury through compression.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Common Fibular (Peroneal) Nerve:** A branch of the sciatic nerve (L4-S2). It arises in the posterior thigh, descends laterally, and passes around the posterior aspect of the fibular head/neck.
- **Fibular Neck Anatomy:** The common fibular nerve winds laterally around the fibular neck, deep to the fascia lata and peroneus longus muscle. This superficial course makes it vulnerable to direct trauma (fracture) or compression (tight cast).
- **Branches of the Common Fibular Nerve:** Just distal to the fibular neck, the common fibular nerve divides into two main branches:
    - **Deep Fibular (Peroneal) Nerve:** Enters the anterior compartment of the leg via the interosseous membrane. It innervates the anterior compartment muscles (tibialis anterior, extensor hallucis longus, extensor digitorum longus, peroneus tertius) responsible for dorsiflexion and toe extension. It also provides sensory innervation to the web space between the first and second toes.
    - **Superficial Fibular (Peroneal) Nerve:** Travels superficially in the lateral compartment, innervating the peroneus longus and peroneus brevis muscles (responsible for eversion). It provides sensory innervation to the lateral aspect of the lower leg and the dorsum of the foot (except the first web space).
- **Clinical Correlation:**
    - **Foot Drop:** Indicates weakness of dorsiflexion, implicating the muscles innervated by the deep fibular nerve (tibialis anterior, etc.).
    - **Sensory Loss (Lateral Leg & Dorsum of Foot):** Corresponds to the sensory distribution of the superficial fibular nerve.
    - **Eversion Weakness:** Indicates weakness of the peroneus longus and brevis muscles, implicating the superficial fibular nerve.
- **Conclusion:** The patient presents with deficits consistent with injury to *both* the deep and superficial branches of the common fibular nerve. Since the common fibular nerve itself is vulnerable at the fibular neck, injury here often affects both branches. The question asks which structure is *most likely compromised*. The common fibular nerve at the fibular neck is the primary site of injury given the fracture location and subsequent cast compression, leading to the observed downstream deficits in its branches. Option (D) specifically refers to the superficial branch, which accounts for the sensory loss and eversion weakness. While the deep branch is also affected (foot drop), the superficial branch's sensory distribution matches the described numbness pattern, and the combination of sensory and motor deficits points to injury of the common nerve or its branches at the fibular neck. Given the options, the superficial fibular nerve is the most specific answer that explains the sensory loss pattern and eversion weakness, both resulting from injury at the fibular neck.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Tibial nerve:** This nerve passes posterior to the medial malleolus and innervates the posterior compartment muscles of the leg (plantarflexion, inversion) and provides sensation to the sole of the foot. Injury would cause plantarflexion weakness and loss of sensation on the plantar surface, which is inconsistent with the patient's findings (foot drop, lateral leg/dorsum sensation loss).
- (B) **Sural nerve:** This is a purely sensory nerve formed by branches of the tibial and common fibular nerves. It provides sensation to the posterolateral aspect of the lower leg and the lateral aspect of the foot/ankle. Injury would cause sensory loss in its distribution, but not motor weakness like foot drop or eversion weakness.
- (C) **Deep fibular (peroneal) nerve:** This nerve innervates the anterior compartment muscles responsible for dorsiflexion and toe extension, and provides sensation to the first web space. Injury would cause foot drop and sensory loss in the first web space. While the patient has foot drop, the sensory loss is described over the lateral leg and dorsum of the foot, not the first web space. Also, eversion weakness is present, which is not innervated by the deep fibular nerve. Injury to the common fibular nerve at the fibular neck typically affects both deep and superficial branches.
- (E) **Sciatic nerve:** This nerve is located in the posterior thigh. Injury typically occurs higher up (e.g., hip fracture, intramuscular injection) and would cause deficits in both the tibial and common fibular nerve distributions (affecting hamstring function, plantarflexion, dorsiflexion, inversion, eversion, and sensation in the posterior thigh, leg, and foot). The patient's deficits are localized to the common fibular nerve distribution in the lower leg and foot, making sciatic nerve injury unlikely given the fibular neck fracture.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Structure                     | Innervation (Motor)                                  | Innervation (Sensory)                                     | Location of Injury (Common Site) | Clinical Findings (Typical)                                                                 |
| :---------------------------- | :--------------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------ | :------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ |
| **Common Fibular Nerve**      | Deep & Superficial Fibular Nerves                    | None (sensory branches arise distally)                   | Fibular Neck                    | Foot drop, sensory loss lateral leg/dorsum foot, eversion weakness (if branches affected) |
| Tibial Nerve                  | Posterior compartment muscles (plantarflexors, invertors) | Sole of foot, posterior leg                             | Popliteal fossa, tarsal tunnel | Plantarflexion weakness, inversion weakness, loss of sensation on sole                       |
| Sural Nerve                   | None (sensory only)                                 | Posterolateral lower leg, lateral foot/ankle             | Variable                        | Sensory loss in distribution                                                                |
| Deep Fibular Nerve            | Anterior compartment muscles (dorsiflexors, extensors) | First web space                                          | Distal to fibular neck          | Foot drop, weakness of toe extension, sensory loss in first web space                         |
| Superficial Fibular Nerve     | Lateral compartment muscles (evertors)               | Lateral lower leg, dorsum of foot (except 1st web space) | Distal to fibular neck          | Eversion weakness, sensory loss lateral leg/dorsum foot                                      |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The common fibular (peroneal) nerve is vulnerable to injury as it wraps around the fibular neck. Fractures of the fibular neck or compression (e.g., tight casts, prolonged leg crossing) can damage the nerve or its branches. Injury results in foot drop (deep branch) and sensory loss over the lateral leg/dorsum of the foot, along with eversion weakness (superficial branch). Differentiating between common fibular nerve injury and tibial nerve injury is crucial based on the pattern of motor and sensory deficits. Remember the mnemonic "DEAD CAT" for deep fibular nerve sensory distribution (Dorsal web space between the first and second toes).

<a id='question-098'></a>

### Question 098: Common Fibular Nerve Palsy
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The common fibular (peroneal) nerve's anatomical course around the fibular neck, its branching into superficial and deep fibular nerves, and the resulting motor deficits (foot drop, loss of eversion) following injury at this location. (胫总神经在腓骨颈处的解剖走行、分支为浅腓神经和深腓神经、以及在此处损伤后的运动功能障碍（足下垂、外翻丧失）。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old male construction worker presents to the emergency department after sustaining a direct blow to the lateral aspect of his left knee while working. He reports immediate onset of pain and numbness in the lateral lower leg and dorsum of the foot. Physical examination reveals significant weakness in dorsiflexion of the foot and ankle (medical grade 3/5), inability to evert the foot against resistance, and diminished sensation over the lateral lower leg and dorsum of the foot. The patient is able to plantarflex the foot normally. Ankle reflexes (Achilles) are intact bilaterally. A lateral radiograph of the left knee shows no fracture.

Which of the following structures is most likely injured, leading to this patient's presentation?

(A) Tibial nerve
(B) Sural nerve
(C) Deep fibular (peroneal) nerve
(D) Superficial fibular (peroneal) nerve
(E) Sciatic nerve

#### Correct Answer: (D)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Mechanism:** Direct blow to the lateral knee. This points towards injury to structures passing superficially around the fibular head/neck.
- **Symptoms:** Pain and numbness in the lateral lower leg and dorsum of the foot. This sensory distribution corresponds to the superficial and deep fibular nerves.
- **Motor Deficits:** Weakness in dorsiflexion (foot drop) and inability to evert the foot. Dorsiflexion is primarily innervated by the deep fibular nerve (tibialis anterior, extensor hallucis longus, extensor digitorum longus). Eversion is primarily innervated by the superficial fibular nerve (peroneus longus, peroneus brevis).
- **Preserved Function:** Normal plantarflexion indicates the tibial nerve is intact. Intact Achilles reflex also supports tibial nerve integrity.
- **Imaging:** No fracture rules out bony injury as the primary cause, focusing attention on soft tissue structures.
- **Conclusion:** The combination of lateral knee trauma, sensory loss in the lateral leg/dorsum of foot, and specific motor deficits (foot drop + loss of eversion) strongly implicates injury to the common fibular nerve or its branches as they wrap around the fibular neck. Since both dorsiflexion (deep branch) and eversion (superficial branch) are affected, the injury likely occurred proximal to the bifurcation of the common fibular nerve, or involved the common fibular nerve itself. Option (D) specifically names the superficial fibular nerve, which is responsible for eversion and contributes to sensation on the dorsum of the foot. Injury to the common fibular nerve would affect both superficial and deep branches, causing the described symptoms. However, among the choices, the superficial fibular nerve is the most specific structure responsible for the loss of eversion, a key finding. If the injury was *only* to the deep fibular nerve (C), eversion would be spared. If the injury was to the common fibular nerve itself, it would encompass the deficits of both branches. Given the options, the superficial fibular nerve is the most likely *specific* structure injured to cause loss of eversion, and the common fibular nerve injury would also cause this. The question asks which structure is *most likely* injured. Injury to the common fibular nerve at the fibular head is the classic cause of this presentation. The superficial fibular nerve is a major branch affected in such an injury.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Common Fibular Nerve (Peroneal Nerve):** A terminal branch of the sciatic nerve (L4-S2). It arises in the posterior thigh, descends laterally, and passes posterior to the knee joint.
- **Course around Fibular Neck:** It winds laterally around the neck of the fibula, deep to the fascia but superficial to the peroneus longus muscle. This superficial course makes it vulnerable to direct trauma, compression (e.g., leg crossing), or fractures of the fibular neck.
- **Bifurcation:** Just distal to the fibular neck, the common fibular nerve divides into the superficial fibular nerve and the deep fibular nerve.
- **Superficial Fibular Nerve:** Runs anteriorly into the lateral compartment of the leg. It innervates the peroneus longus and peroneus brevis muscles (responsible for eversion) and provides sensory innervation to the lateral leg and dorsum of the foot (excluding the web space between the 1st and 2nd toes).
- **Deep Fibular Nerve:** Runs anteriorly into the anterior compartment of the leg, deep to the extensor retinaculum. It innervates the tibialis anterior, extensor hallucis longus, extensor digitorum longus, and peroneus tertius muscles (responsible for dorsiflexion and toe extension). It provides sensory innervation to the web space between the 1st and 2nd toes.
- **Clinical Correlation:** Injury to the common fibular nerve at the fibular neck results in paralysis of both the superficial and deep fibular nerve distributions. This manifests as foot drop (inability to dorsiflex due to deep fibular nerve injury) and loss of eversion (inability to evert due to superficial fibular nerve injury). Sensory loss occurs over the lateral leg and dorsum of the foot. The tibial nerve, responsible for plantarflexion and sensation on the sole of the foot, is unaffected.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Tibial nerve:** This nerve descends posteriorly in the leg, deep within the posterior compartment. It innervates the plantarflexors (gastrocnemius, soleus, plantaris, popliteus, tibialis posterior, flexor hallucis longus, flexor digitorum longus) and provides sensation to the sole of the foot. Injury would cause inability to plantarflex and sensory loss on the sole, which is inconsistent with the patient's presentation (normal plantarflexion, sensory loss on dorsum/lateral leg).
- (B) **Sural nerve:** This is a purely sensory nerve formed by branches of the tibial and common fibular nerves. It runs superficially down the posterior aspect of the leg, curving around the lateral malleolus to supply sensation to the posterolateral aspect of the lower leg and the lateral aspect of the foot. Injury would cause sensory loss in its distribution but no motor deficits, which is inconsistent with the patient's motor weakness.
- (C) **Deep fibular (peroneal) nerve:** This nerve is a branch of the common fibular nerve. It innervates the anterior compartment muscles responsible for dorsiflexion. Injury *only* to the deep fibular nerve would cause foot drop (loss of dorsiflexion) and sensory loss in the first web space, but eversion would be preserved (innervated by the superficial fibular nerve). The patient has loss of *both* dorsiflexion and eversion, suggesting a more proximal injury affecting both branches or the common nerve itself. While injury to the deep branch is part of the overall picture, the loss of eversion points to involvement of the superficial branch or the common nerve.
- (E) **Sciatic nerve:** This is the parent nerve of both the tibial and common fibular nerves. Injury typically occurs in the posterior thigh or buttock. Injury high up would affect both tibial and common fibular nerve distributions, leading to foot drop, loss of eversion, loss of plantarflexion, and sensory loss over the entire lower leg and foot. The patient has normal plantarflexion, ruling out a high sciatic nerve injury.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Structure                 | Innervation (Motor)                                  | Sensory Distribution                                  | Location of Injury (Typical) | Key Deficit(s) in Injury                                  |
| :------------------------ | :--------------------------------------------------- | :---------------------------------------------------- | :--------------------------- | :-------------------------------------------------------- |
| **Common Fibular Nerve** | Deep & Superficial Fibular Nerves                   | Lateral leg, dorsum of foot (via branches)           | Fibular neck                 | Foot drop, loss of eversion, sensory loss (lateral leg/dorsum) |
| **Superficial Fibular N.** | Peroneus longus, brevis (Eversion)                 | Lateral leg, dorsum of foot (excl. 1st web space)    | Anterior leg                 | Loss of eversion, sensory loss (lateral leg/dorsum)       |
| **Deep Fibular N.**       | Tibialis anterior, EDL, EHL, Peroneus tertius (Dorsiflexion) | Web space between 1st and 2nd toes                   | Anterior leg                 | Foot drop, sensory loss (1st web space)                  |
| **Tibial Nerve**          | Plantarflexors, intrinsic foot muscles              | Sole of foot, posterior leg                           | Posterior leg                | Loss of plantarflexion, sensory loss (sole)               |
| **Sural Nerve**           | None (Sensory only)                                 | Posterolateral leg, lateral foot                      | Posterior leg                | Sensory loss only (posterolateral leg/lateral foot)       |
| **Sciatic Nerve**         | Tibial & Common Fibular Nerves                       | Entire lower leg and foot                             | Buttock/Posterior Thigh      | Foot drop, loss of eversion, loss of plantarflexion, widespread sensory loss |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The common fibular nerve is vulnerable to injury as it wraps around the fibular neck. Injury here typically causes foot drop (deep fibular nerve) and loss of eversion (superficial fibular nerve) due to paralysis of the anterior and lateral compartment muscles, respectively. Differentiating injuries to the common fibular nerve, superficial fibular nerve, and deep fibular nerve relies on identifying the specific motor and sensory deficits. Remember that the tibial nerve innervates plantarflexion and the sole of the foot.

### Question 099: Steppage Gait
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Steppage gait (high-stepping gait) is a compensatory mechanism for foot drop, which results from weakness or paralysis of the dorsiflexors of the foot, primarily innervated by the deep fibular (peroneal) nerve. (步态障碍：步高抬腿，代偿足下垂，足下垂由足背屈肌群无力/瘫痪引起，主要由胫腓神经深支支配。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 55-year-old male presents to the emergency department after sustaining a closed fracture of the proximal fibula while skiing. On examination, he complains of numbness and tingling along the lateral aspect of his lower leg and dorsum of his foot. Motor examination reveals an inability to dorsiflex his right foot against resistance, and he cannot maintain a neutral position when asked to stand on his toes (positive Romberg sign when standing on toes). When asked to walk, he demonstrates a characteristic high-stepping gait, lifting his right leg excessively high with each step to clear the ground. Sensory testing shows decreased sensation over the web space between his first and second toes. Which of the following structures is most likely injured, leading to this patient's presentation?

(A) Tibial nerve
(B) Superficial fibular (peroneal) nerve
(C) Common fibular (peroneal) nerve
(D) Sural nerve
(E) Saphenous nerve

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Mechanism of Injury:** Closed fracture of the proximal fibula. This location is critical as the common fibular (peroneal) nerve wraps around the neck of the fibula.
- **Sensory Symptoms:** Numbness and tingling along the lateral aspect of the lower leg and dorsum of the foot. This sensory distribution corresponds to the territory of the common fibular (peroneal) nerve and its branches.
- **Motor Deficit:** Inability to dorsiflex the foot against resistance. Dorsiflexion is primarily performed by the tibialis anterior, extensor hallucis longus, and extensor digitorum longus muscles, all innervated by the deep fibular (peroneal) nerve.
- **Gait Abnormality:** High-stepping gait (steppage gait). This is a compensatory mechanism to prevent the foot from dragging during the swing phase of walking, characteristic of foot drop caused by dorsiflexor weakness.
- **Specific Sensory Loss:** Decreased sensation over the web space between the first and second toes. This specific area is innervated by the deep fibular (peroneal) nerve.
- **Positive Romberg sign when standing on toes:** Inability to maintain balance when standing on toes indicates weakness of the plantar flexors (gastrocnemius, soleus), which are innervated by the tibial nerve. However, the primary presentation is foot drop (dorsiflexor weakness), and the injury location (proximal fibula) strongly points towards the common fibular nerve. The inability to stand on toes might be due to pain or secondary effects, but the foot drop and high-stepping gait are the dominant features.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Common Fibular (Peroneal) Nerve:** This is a branch of the sciatic nerve (L4-S2). It arises in the posterior thigh, descends laterally, and wraps around the neck of the fibula just distal to the fibular head. This superficial course makes it vulnerable to injury with proximal fibular fractures.
- **Branches of the Common Fibular Nerve:** Just after wrapping around the fibular neck, the common fibular nerve divides into the superficial fibular (peroneal) nerve and the deep fibular (peroneal) nerve.
    - **Superficial Fibular Nerve:** Innervates the lateral compartment muscles (fibularis longus and brevis) responsible for eversion, and provides sensory innervation to the anterolateral aspect of the lower leg and the dorsum of the foot (except the web space between the 1st and 2nd toes).
    - **Deep Fibular Nerve:** Innervates the anterior compartment muscles (tibialis anterior, extensor hallucis longus, extensor digitorum longus, fibularis tertius) responsible for dorsiflexion and toe extension, and provides sensory innervation to the web space between the 1st and 2nd toes.
- **Clinical Correlation:** Injury to the common fibular nerve at the fibular neck affects both its superficial and deep branches. This results in:
    - **Motor:** Weakness of dorsiflexion (deep branch) and eversion (superficial branch), leading to foot drop and difficulty walking on uneven surfaces.
    - **Sensory:** Loss of sensation over the anterolateral lower leg and dorsum of the foot (superficial branch) and the web space between the 1st and 2nd toes (deep branch).
    - **Gait:** Steppage gait (high-stepping) to compensate for foot drop.
- **Tibial Nerve:** Arises from the sciatic nerve, descends posteriorly in the leg, and innervates the posterior compartment muscles (plantarflexors, invertors) and provides sensory innervation to the sole of the foot. Injury causes plantarflexion weakness and loss of sensation on the sole.
- **Superficial Fibular Nerve:** Injury causes eversion weakness and sensory loss over the anterolateral leg and dorsum of the foot (excluding the 1st web space). Does not cause significant foot drop.
- **Sural Nerve:** Formed by branches of the tibial and common fibular nerves. Provides sensory innervation to the posterolateral aspect of the lower leg and the lateral aspect of the foot. Injury does not cause motor weakness or foot drop.
- **Saphenous Nerve:** A branch of the femoral nerve. Provides sensory innervation to the medial aspect of the lower leg and foot. Injury does not cause motor weakness or foot drop.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Tibial nerve**: Incorrect. The tibial nerve innervates the posterior compartment muscles (plantarflexors) and the sole of the foot. Injury would cause inability to plantarflex or stand on toes, loss of sensation on the sole, and potentially a foot inversion deformity. This patient has foot drop (dorsiflexion weakness) and sensory loss on the dorsum of the foot.
- (B) **Superficial fibular (peroneal) nerve**: Incorrect. Injury to the superficial fibular nerve would cause weakness of foot eversion (fibularis longus and brevis) and sensory loss over the anterolateral lower leg and dorsum of the foot, *excluding* the web space between the first and second toes. While it contributes to the sensory loss pattern, it does not explain the significant foot drop (dorsiflexion weakness) or the sensory loss in the specific web space, nor does it fully account for the injury mechanism at the proximal fibula (where the common fibular nerve wraps around).
- (D) **Sural nerve**: Incorrect. The sural nerve is primarily sensory, innervating the posterolateral lower leg and lateral foot. Injury would cause sensory loss in this distribution but no motor weakness or foot drop.
- (E) **Saphenous nerve**: Incorrect. The saphenous nerve is sensory, innervating the medial lower leg and foot. Injury would cause sensory loss in this distribution but no motor weakness or foot drop.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):
| Feature             | Common Fibular Nerve Injury (Proximal Fibula) | Tibial Nerve Injury | Superficial Fibular Nerve Injury | Sural Nerve Injury | Saphenous Nerve Injury |
|----------------------|-------------------------------------------------|----------------------|-----------------------------------|--------------------|-----------------------|
| **Motor Deficit**    | Foot drop (dorsiflexion weakness), Eversion weakness | Plantarflexion weakness | Eversion weakness                 | None               | None                  |
| **Sensory Deficit** | Lateral leg, Dorsum foot, 1st web space        | Sole of foot         | Anterolateral leg, Dorsum foot (excl. 1st web space) | Posterolateral leg, Lateral foot | Medial leg, Medial foot |
| **Gait**             | Steppage gait                                  | Difficulty with heel raise | Difficulty with eversion          | Normal             | Normal                |
| **Injury Location**  | Proximal Fibula Neck                          | Popliteal fossa, Tarsal tunnel | Distal leg                       | Distal leg         | Medial leg            |
| **Muscles Affected** | Tibialis anterior, Extensors, Peroneus longus/brevis | Gastrocnemius, Soleus, Tibialis posterior, Invertors | Peroneus longus/brevis | None                  | None                  |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Steppage gait is a classic sign of foot drop, caused by weakness of the dorsiflexors of the foot. The most common cause is injury to the common fibular (peroneal) nerve, particularly at the fibular neck due to fractures. Differentiating between common fibular, superficial fibular, and tibial nerve injuries relies on understanding their specific motor and sensory distributions. Remember the mnemonic "DAVE" for the deep fibular nerve: Dorsiflexion, toe Extension, sensory to the first Web space. The superficial fibular nerve handles Eversion and sensation elsewhere on the dorsum/lateral leg. The tibial nerve handles plantarflexion and sensation on the sole.

<a id='question-100'></a>

### Question 100: Superficial Posterior Compartment of the Leg
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The superficial posterior compartment of the leg contains the gastrocnemius, soleus, and plantaris muscles, innervated by the tibial nerve (S1-S2). Understanding the anatomical relationships, actions, and potential injuries within this compartment is crucial. (腿后侧浅层间室包含比目鱼肌、腓肠肌和股薄肌，由胫神经（S1-S2）支配。理解此间室内的解剖关系、功能和潜在损伤至关重要。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 68-year-old male presents to the emergency department after sustaining a penetrating injury to his posterior lower leg during a fall onto a garden rake. On examination, he has significant pain and swelling in the posterior calf region. Palpation reveals a palpable defect just superior and lateral to the medial malleolus. Motor examination shows intact dorsiflexion and eversion but marked weakness in plantarflexion. Sensory examination reveals intact sensation on the dorsum of the foot but loss of sensation on the lateral aspect of the foot and heel. The Achilles tendon reflex is absent bilaterally. Ankle-brachial index (ABI) is normal.

Which of the following structures is MOST likely injured, explaining the patient's clinical presentation?

(A) Deep peroneal nerve as it passes through the fibular tunnel.
(B) Tibial nerve as it passes between the heads of the gastrocnemius muscle.
(C) Posterior tibial artery as it passes through the tarsal tunnel.
(D) Sural nerve as it passes posterior to the lateral malleolus.
(E) Common peroneal nerve as it winds around the fibular neck.

#### Correct Answer: (B)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Anatomical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Injury Mechanism:** Penetrating injury to the posterior lower leg suggests damage to structures in the posterior compartments.
- **Palpable Defect:** Location superior and lateral to the medial malleolus points towards the posterior aspect of the distal leg, near the origin of the Achilles tendon.
- **Motor Deficit:** Weakness in plantarflexion implicates the muscles responsible for this action (gastrocnemius and soleus) and their innervation.
- **Sensory Deficit:** Loss of sensation on the lateral foot and heel corresponds to the sensory distribution of the sural nerve (derived from medial sural cutaneous nerve from tibial nerve and lateral sural cutaneous nerve from common peroneal nerve).
- **Reflex Deficit:** Absent Achilles tendon reflex indicates disruption of the tibial nerve pathway, which mediates this reflex.
- **ABI Normal:** Rules out significant arterial injury affecting overall limb perfusion.

These clues collectively point to an injury affecting the tibial nerve and potentially the muscles it innervates (gastrocnemius, soleus) and the structures providing sensation to the lateral foot/heel (sural nerve branches). The location of the injury (posterior calf, near medial malleolus) and the specific deficits (plantarflexion weakness, absent Achilles reflex, lateral foot/heel sensory loss) strongly suggest damage to the tibial nerve in the superficial posterior compartment, specifically as it passes between the heads of the gastrocnemius.

##### 2. Anatomical, Embryological & Clinical Deep-Dive (解剖毗邻、神经血管支配、胚胎发生与临床相关性深度剖析)
- **Superficial Posterior Compartment:** Contains gastrocnemius, soleus, and plantaris muscles.
- **Tibial Nerve:** Enters the leg posteriorly, deep to the soleus. It runs down the posterior compartment, initially deep to the soleus, then passes superficially between the two heads of the gastrocnemius muscle. It is accompanied by the posterior tibial artery and veins.
- **Innervation:** The tibial nerve (L4-S3, primarily S1-S2) innervates the gastrocnemius, soleus, and plantaris muscles (responsible for plantarflexion). It also provides sensory innervation to the posterior leg and the sole of the foot via its branches (medial and lateral plantar nerves, medial sural cutaneous nerve).
- **Sural Nerve:** Provides sensory innervation to the posterolateral aspect of the lower leg and the lateral aspect of the foot and heel. It is formed by branches from the tibial nerve (medial sural cutaneous nerve) and the common peroneal nerve (lateral sural cutaneous nerve). Injury to the tibial nerve can affect the medial sural cutaneous branch, contributing to sensory loss in the sural nerve distribution.
- **Achilles Tendon Reflex:** A stretch reflex mediated by the tibial nerve (afferent and efferent limbs). Damage to the tibial nerve disrupts this reflex arc.
- **Location of Injury:** The described injury location (posterior calf, defect near medial malleolus) aligns perfectly with the course of the tibial nerve as it passes between the heads of the gastrocnemius, just proximal to its bifurcation into medial and lateral plantar nerves. Damage here would affect plantarflexion (gastrocnemius, soleus), the Achilles reflex, and potentially sensory branches like the medial sural cutaneous nerve.

##### 3. Embryological Origin:
- The muscles of the superficial posterior compartment (gastrocnemius, soleus, plantaris) develop from somites (myotome).
- The tibial nerve originates from the sciatic nerve, which itself arises from the lumbosacral plexus (L4-S3 nerve roots).

##### 4. Vascular Supply:
- The posterior tibial artery runs with the tibial nerve through the posterior compartment. While the ABI was normal, a localized injury could still affect the vasa nervorum supplying the nerve itself.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Deep peroneal nerve as it passes through the fibular tunnel:** The deep peroneal nerve is located in the anterior compartment of the leg, running laterally around the fibular neck and then deep within the fibular tunnel. Injury here causes foot drop (weakness of dorsiflexion and toe extension) and sensory loss on the web space between the first and second toes. This does not match the patient's plantarflexion weakness and sensory loss pattern.
- (C) **Posterior tibial artery as it passes through the tarsal tunnel:** The posterior tibial artery passes through the tarsal tunnel (behind the medial malleolus) before dividing into the medial and lateral plantar arteries. While injury here could cause ischemia to the foot, the ABI was normal, and the primary deficits described are neurological (motor weakness, sensory loss, reflex loss), not vascular compromise affecting the entire limb. Furthermore, the injury location described is more proximal in the calf.
- (D) **Sural nerve as it passes posterior to the lateral malleolus:** The sural nerve runs down the posterolateral aspect of the leg and passes posterior to the lateral malleolus. Injury here would cause sensory loss over the lateral aspect of the foot and ankle but would not affect motor function (plantarflexion) or the Achilles reflex, as it is purely sensory. The injury location described is more medial and proximal.
- (E) **Common peroneal nerve as it winds around the fibular neck:** The common peroneal nerve is located in the lateral compartment of the leg. Injury here (e.g., from fracture or compression) causes foot drop (weakness of dorsiflexion and eversion) and sensory loss over the anterolateral aspect of the lower leg and dorsum of the foot. This does not match the patient's plantarflexion weakness and sensory loss pattern.

#### High-Yield Anatomical Comparison Table (重点解剖结构与易混淆点横向对比):

| Structure                     | Location                                     | Motor Function Affected | Sensory Loss Area              | Reflex Affected | Key Anatomical Relationship                                  |
| :---------------------------- | :------------------------------------------- | :---------------------- | :----------------------------- | :------------- | :---------------------------------------------------------- |
| **Tibial Nerve (Correct)**    | Posterior compartment, between gastrocnemius heads | Plantarflexion          | Lateral foot/heel (sural), sole | Achilles       | Runs with posterior tibial artery; bifurcates into plantar nerves |
| Deep Peroneal Nerve (A)       | Anterior compartment, fibular tunnel         | Dorsiflexion, eversion  | 1st web space                  | None           | Runs deep to extensor muscles                               |
| Posterior Tibial Artery (C)   | Posterior compartment, tarsal tunnel         | None (vascular)         | None (vascular)                 | None           | Runs with tibial nerve                                      |
| Sural Nerve (D)               | Posterolateral leg, posterior lateral malleolus | None                   | Lateral foot/heel               | None           | Formed by branches of tibial and common peroneal nerves      |
| Common Peroneal Nerve (E)     | Lateral compartment, fibular neck            | Dorsiflexion, eversion  | Anterolateral leg, dorsum foot | None           | Wraps around fibular neck; divides into superficial/deep branches |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The tibial nerve runs down the posterior compartment of the leg, passing between the heads of the gastrocnemius muscle before bifurcating. Injury here causes weakness in plantarflexion (gastrocnemius, soleus), loss of the Achilles reflex, and sensory loss in its distribution (including the lateral foot/heel via the sural nerve branches). Differentiating this from injuries to the common peroneal nerve (foot drop, dorsum sensory loss) or deep peroneal nerve (foot drop, 1st web space sensory loss) is crucial based on the specific motor and sensory deficits.
